### Towards Explainable Temporal User Profiling with LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.00886v1)
- **Authors**: Milad Sabouri, Masoud Mansoury, Kun Lin, Bamshad Mobasher
- **Abstract**: Accurately modeling user preferences is vital not only for improving recommendation performance but also for enhancing transparency in recommender systems. Conventional user profiling methods, such as averaging item embeddings, often overlook the evolving, nuanced nature of user interests, particularly the interplay between short-term and long-term preferences. In this work, we leverage large language models (LLMs) to generate natural language summaries of users' interaction histories, distinguishing recent behaviors from more persistent tendencies. Our framework not only models temporal user preferences but also produces natural language profiles that can be used to explain recommendations in an interpretable manner. These textual profiles are encoded via a pre-trained model, and an attention mechanism dynamically fuses the short-term and long-term embeddings into a comprehensive user representation. Beyond boosting recommendation accuracy over multiple baselines, our approach naturally supports explainability: the interpretable text summaries and attention weights can be exposed to end users, offering insights into why specific items are suggested. Experiments on real-world datasets underscore both the performance gains and the promise of generating clearer, more transparent justifications for content-based recommendations.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Towards Explainable Temporal User Profiling with LLMs":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel approach to user profiling for content-based recommender systems that leverages Large Language Models (LLMs) to generate explainable profiles reflecting both short-term and long-term user interests. The method transforms user interaction histories into two textual profiles using distinct prompts. These profiles are then encoded into embeddings and fused using an attention mechanism to create a comprehensive user representation. The key idea is to use the LLM-generated text as a built-in explanation, providing transparency for why specific items are recommended. Experiments on Amazon product review datasets (Movies&TV and Video Games) demonstrate improved ranking performance and highlight the method's suitability for explaining recommendations based on the interplay of recent and enduring preferences.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper introduces a compelling combination of techniques, integrating LLMs for explainable user profiling with a temporal modeling approach. While the individual components (LLMs, attention mechanisms, temporal segmentation) are not entirely novel, their synergistic integration within the recommendation framework and the specific design of the LLM prompts for short-term and long-term interest extraction does represent a novel contribution. The direct use of LLM generated text as explanation offers a distinct benefit over other explanation generating techniques, such as attention weights from a transformer-based model.

*   **Significance:** The work addresses a critical need for explainability in recommender systems, moving beyond simple averaging of item embeddings to capture evolving user interests and provide interpretable rationales. The experimental results, particularly on the Movies&TV dataset, are promising. The differentiation between short-term and long-term interests has practical implications, leading to better recommendations, and allowing users to understand why items are suggested. The framework provides insights into model behavior, which is beneficial for developers. The potential to address the "cold start" problem is a promising avenue for future work.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Explainability:** The core strength is the focus on generating inherently interpretable user profiles using LLMs. The textual summaries directly provide reasons for recommendations, addressing a key limitation of many existing approaches.
    *   **Temporal Modeling:** The differentiation between short-term and long-term preferences allows for a more nuanced understanding of user interests, improving recommendation accuracy.
    *   **Experimental Validation:** The experiments on two datasets with different user profile characteristics provide valuable insights into the method's strengths and weaknesses.
    *   **Ablation Study:** The ablation study effectively isolates the contributions of each component, demonstrating the importance of LLM-based text, temporal segmentation, attention, and the non-linear MLP.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Dataset Dependency:** The results indicate that the method performs better on datasets with larger and more dynamic user profiles (Movies&TV). Performance gains are less significant on the dataset with steadier profiles (Video Games), suggesting a dependency on the richness of user data. The limitations with smaller user profiles could be addressed with strategies such as prompt engineering that encourages the LLM to augment the existing profiles, or with data augmentation.
    *   **Computational Cost:** Using LLMs can be computationally expensive, particularly during inference. While the paper doesn't explicitly address this, it's a potential limitation for real-world deployment.
    *   **Subjectivity of Explanations:** The quality and relevance of the generated explanations depend on the choice of prompts and the LLM's ability to capture user interests accurately. There might be subjectivity in how the LLM interprets user interactions and generates explanations. A user study measuring the quality of the explanations from human perspective would be helpful in validating the approach.

*   **Potential Impact:** The paper could significantly impact the design of content-based recommender systems by demonstrating the value of LLMs for explainable user profiling. The idea of using temporal signals and natural language explanations can be extended to other recommendation scenarios and datasets.

**Justification for Score:**

Considering the strengths and weaknesses, the paper presents a novel approach to explainable user profiling and provides promising experimental results. While the performance is dataset-dependent and computational cost is a valid concern, the potential benefits of interpretability and improved accuracy justify a positive evaluation. The weaknesses are identified and could lead to further improvements in future research.

**Score: 7.5**

- **Classification**: cs.IR
- **Score**: 7/10

### NeMo-Inspector: A Visualization Tool for LLM Generation Analysis
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.00903v1)
- **Authors**: Daria Gitman, Igor Gitman, Evelina Bakhturina
- **Abstract**: Adapting Large Language Models (LLMs) to novel tasks and enhancing their overall capabilities often requires large, high-quality training datasets. Synthetic data, generated at scale, serves a valuable alternative when real-world data is scarce or difficult to obtain. However, ensuring the quality of synthetic datasets is challenging, as developers must manually inspect and refine numerous samples to identify errors and areas for improvement. This process is time-consuming and requires specialized tools. We introduce NeMo-Inspector, an open-source tool designed to simplify the analysis of synthetic datasets with integrated inference capabilities. We demonstrate its effectiveness through two real-world cases. Analysis and cleaning of the synthetically generated GSM-Plus dataset with NeMo-Inspector led to a significant decrease in low-quality samples from 46.99% to 19.51%. The tool also helped identify and correct generation errors in OpenMath models, improving accuracy by 1.92% on the MATH dataset and by 4.17% on the GSM8K dataset for a Meta-Llama-3-8B model fine-tuned on synthetic data generated from Nemotron-4-340B.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces NeMo-Inspector, an open-source visualization tool designed to aid in the analysis of Large Language Model (LLM) generations, particularly focusing on synthetic datasets. The tool provides features such as support for different types of generations (homogeneous and heterogeneous), custom Python functions for data manipulation, manual editing capabilities, streamlined inference with support for various backends (NeMo, TensorRT-LLM, vLLM, OpenAI API), and flexible data visualization with Markdown, LaTeX, and Python syntax highlighting.  It offers three perspectives for data analysis: Intra-Sample, Comparative, and Inter-Parameter. The paper showcases the utility of NeMo-Inspector through case studies involving the cleaning of a synthetic GSM-Plus dataset and error pattern identification in OpenMath models, leading to improvements in model accuracy on MATH and GSM8K datasets. The paper highlights the tool's effectiveness in identifying and addressing data quality issues and in fine-tuning prompts.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Strengths:**
    * **Practical Utility:** The tool addresses a real need in the LLM development workflow, which is the analysis and improvement of synthetic data.
    * **Comprehensive Feature Set:** NeMo-Inspector integrates several functionalities into a single tool, including inference, analysis, editing, and visualization, making it a more convenient solution compared to using separate tools for each task.  The differentiation between homogeneous and heterogeneous generation analysis is well thought out.
    * **Extensibility:**  The support for custom Python functions enhances the tool's flexibility and allows users to tailor the analysis to their specific needs.
    * **Clear Use Cases:** The paper provides clear examples and case studies demonstrating the tool's effectiveness in improving data quality and identifying model errors. The quantified improvements on GSM-Plus, MATH, and GSM8K provide tangible evidence.
    * **Open Source:** Being open-source promotes wider adoption and community contributions.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Limited Novelty:** While the tool integrates several useful features, many of these features already exist in other data analysis tools (as the paper acknowledges in Table 1). The primary novelty lies in the *integration* of these features into a single LLM-focused tool, not in the invention of fundamentally new analysis techniques.
    * **JSONL Restriction:**  The restriction to JSONL format limits its applicability to datasets in other formats, requiring preprocessing.
    * **Security Concerns:** The execution of user-provided Python code presents potential security risks, especially in a collaborative or public setting. The paper acknowledges this, but offers no specific mitigation strategy.
    * **Dependency on NeMo-Skills:** The reliance on NeMo-Skills scripts for inference limits the range of models that can be easily analyzed. This creates a barrier to entry for users not already invested in the NeMo ecosystem.  While NeMo is powerful, this requirement limits the tool's broader applicability.
    * **Limited Evaluation:** Although two case studies are provided, they heavily focus on mathematical problems. Evaluation on a broader set of tasks and datasets would strengthen the paper.

* **Significance:**

The tool has the potential to significantly improve the efficiency of synthetic data analysis and model debugging for LLMs.  While it doesn't introduce groundbreaking novel techniques, its integration of various functions and streamlined workflow could save developers time and effort.  The open-source nature and the possibility of community contributions will ensure continuous improvements and widespread adoption.  However, the JSONL and NeMo-Skills restrictions diminish the potential reach.

**Justification for Score:**

The score reflects a balance between the tool's practical utility and its limitations in terms of novelty and broader applicability. While NeMo-Inspector addresses an important challenge in LLM development and offers a comprehensive set of features, its limited novelty and dependencies on NeMo-Skills ecosystem, JSONL structure and security issues hinder its broader significance. Therefore, the tool's contribution is incremental rather than revolutionary.

**Score: 6**

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 6/10

### Multivariate Conformal Selection
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.00917v1)
- **Authors**: Tian Bai, Yue Zhao, Xiang Yu, Archer Y. Yang
- **Abstract**: Selecting high-quality candidates from large datasets is critical in applications such as drug discovery, precision medicine, and alignment of large language models (LLMs). While Conformal Selection (CS) provides rigorous uncertainty quantification, it is limited to univariate responses and scalar criteria. To address this issue, we propose Multivariate Conformal Selection (mCS), a generalization of CS designed for multivariate response settings. Our method introduces regional monotonicity and employs multivariate nonconformity scores to construct conformal p-values, enabling finite-sample False Discovery Rate (FDR) control. We present two variants: mCS-dist, using distance-based scores, and mCS-learn, which learns optimal scores via differentiable optimization. Experiments on simulated and real-world datasets demonstrate that mCS significantly improves selection power while maintaining FDR control, establishing it as a robust framework for multivariate selection tasks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and evaluation of the paper "Multivariate Conformal Selection":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Multivariate Conformal Selection (mCS), an extension of Conformal Selection (CS) to handle multivariate response settings.  Traditional CS is limited to univariate responses and scalar criteria, while mCS addresses situations where selection needs to consider multiple interdependent criteria.  The authors propose using regional monotonicity for nonconformity functions and present two variants of mCS: `mCS-dist` (distance-based scores) and `mCS-learn` (learning-based scores).  `mCS-learn` uses a loss function to optimize nonconformity scores.  Experiments on simulated and real-world datasets demonstrate that mCS improves selection power while maintaining finite-sample False Discovery Rate (FDR) control.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The extension of conformal selection to multivariate settings is a significant and valuable contribution. The original conformal selection framework is powerful but limited in scope.  Generalizing this to handle multivariate responses substantially broadens the applicability of the conformal framework to real-world scenarios. The introduction of the concept of regional monotonicity tailored to multivariate settings is a well-motivated novel component of the paper.  The two proposed variants, `mCS-dist` and particularly `mCS-learn`, offer practical implementations with different trade-offs.

*   **Significance:**  The significance lies in the broad applicability of multivariate selection problems. Fields like drug discovery, precision medicine, and large language model alignment routinely involve optimizing based on multiple, often interdependent, criteria.  The ability to provide uncertainty quantification in these settings, while controlling the FDR, is highly desirable. The paper addresses a practical and important need.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Rigorous Framework:**  The paper provides a solid theoretical foundation for mCS, including the proof of finite-sample FDR control.
    *   **Practical Algorithms:**  The two variants, `mCS-dist` and `mCS-learn`, provide concrete implementations and offer flexibility in different scenarios (e.g., regular vs. irregular target regions).
    *   **Empirical Validation:**  The paper includes thorough empirical evaluations on both synthetic and real-world datasets, demonstrating the benefits of mCS over baseline methods. The code/implementation details are present which is crucial.
    *   The discussion of regional monotonicity, compared to standard monotonicity, and how it relates to a real target region, is very strong.
    *   The paper includes theoretical guarantees that the chosen score will still guarantee FDR control.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The computational cost and scalability, particularly of `mCS-learn`, are not thoroughly addressed. Training a model to optimize the nonconformity score could be expensive, especially for high-dimensional data or large datasets.
    *   While the paper compares against adapted baselines, it would be useful to compare with a "true" multivariate CP method directly (even if the objective is not directly aligned). It could further contextualize the advantage mCS has, compared to "generic" CP techniques.
    *   The reliance on a well-fitted underlying predictive model `û` is a potential limitation. While the paper mentions that `mCS-learn` can still perform well when `û` is poorly fitted (see C.2.4), this statement could be stronger to highlight mCS's ability to be robust. How does the model-agnostic-ness of the method play out?
    *   Real-world experiments are only on a singular dataset, a more diverse range of experiments, especially varying the dimension of the responses would have strengthed the work.

*   **Potential Influence:** mCS has the potential to become a valuable tool in various domains where multivariate selection is critical. It can lead to more efficient and reliable decision-making in drug discovery, personalized medicine, and AI alignment. The mCS-learn component may have further impact when paired with better models and further efficiency improvements (since it is a more flexible paradigm).

**Justification for Score:**

While the paper presents a significant and practical extension to conformal selection, the relative novelty is somewhat tempered by the existing literature in multivariate conformal prediction (although not directly aimed at selection). The experiments showcase clear improvements, but limitations around scalability of the learning-based approach, the reliance on an initial model and the extent of real-world experiment limit the overall impact. Therefore, the paper is a solid step forward, deserving of recognition for its clear presentation, rigorous analysis, and practical relevance, but leaving room for further research and development.

### How Transformers Learn Regular Language Recognition: A Theoretical Study on Training Dynamics and Implicit Bias
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.00926v1)
- **Authors**: Ruiquan Huang, Yingbin Liang, Jing Yang
- **Abstract**: Language recognition tasks are fundamental in natural language processing (NLP) and have been widely used to benchmark the performance of large language models (LLMs). These tasks also play a crucial role in explaining the working mechanisms of transformers. In this work, we focus on two representative tasks in the category of regular language recognition, known as `even pairs' and `parity check', the aim of which is to determine whether the occurrences of certain subsequences in a given sequence are even. Our goal is to explore how a one-layer transformer, consisting of an attention layer followed by a linear layer, learns to solve these tasks by theoretically analyzing its training dynamics under gradient descent. While even pairs can be solved directly by a one-layer transformer, parity check need to be solved by integrating Chain-of-Thought (CoT), either into the inference stage of a transformer well-trained for the even pairs task, or into the training of a one-layer transformer. For both problems, our analysis shows that the joint training of attention and linear layers exhibits two distinct phases. In the first phase, the attention layer grows rapidly, mapping data sequences into separable vectors. In the second phase, the attention layer becomes stable, while the linear layer grows logarithmically and approaches in direction to a max-margin hyperplane that correctly separates the attention layer outputs into positive and negative samples, and the loss decreases at a rate of $O(1/t)$. Our experiments validate those theoretical results.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper presents a theoretical analysis of how a one-layer transformer learns to solve regular language recognition tasks, specifically "even pairs" and "parity check". It analyzes the training dynamics under gradient descent, showing two distinct phases: a rapid growth phase where attention and linear layers align, and a slower margin maximization phase driven by implicit bias. The paper also explores how Chain-of-Thought (CoT) can be incorporated, either during inference on a pre-trained model or during training, to solve the more complex "parity check" problem. The analysis includes novel analytical techniques for studying the joint training of attention and linear layers. The theoretical findings are validated through experiments.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper makes several notable contributions, including:
    *   A theoretical study on the training dynamics of transformers for solving the "even pairs" problem, which hadn't been explored previously.
    *   An innovative use of insights from the "even pairs" problem and CoT to address the "parity check" problem in different ways (inference via truncated CoT and training with CoT under teacher forcing). Showing that a transformer trained on one task can solve another without further training through inference tricks is valuable.
    *   A new analytical technique for studying the coupled training of attention and linear layers, employing Taylor expansions and implicit bias principles.
    *   A more general training dynamics characterization compared to prior work, including joint training and convergence analysis.

    While some previous work exists on parity check and related tasks, this paper extends that by analyzing the joint training of attention and linear layers, providing convergence guarantees, and leveraging the connection between "even pairs" and parity check. The most novel part involves using even pair trained transformers for the parity check task with clever inference.

*   **Significance:** The significance stems from providing a theoretical foundation for understanding how transformers learn regular language recognition tasks. This is crucial for understanding the inner workings of these models and could lead to better architecture designs and training strategies. Analyzing the CoT mechanisms further enhances its value.  By giving proofs on convergence etc. it exceeds the "three step" training observations in previous work.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Problem Formulation:** The paper clearly defines the problems and the models used.
    *   **Rigorous Theoretical Analysis:** The analysis is mathematically sound, with clearly stated assumptions and theorems.
    *   **Novel Analytical Techniques:** The Taylor expansion and implicit bias application are valuable contributions.
    *   **Experimental Validation:**  The experiments confirm the theoretical findings.
    * **CoT insights:** Demonstrates that the even-pairs task is deeply connected to parity check and CoT style inference can unlock the more complex problem with no additional training.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Simplicity of the Model:** The use of a one-layer transformer simplifies the analysis but may limit the generalizability of the findings to deeper, more complex architectures. While providing a foundational understanding, the relevance to larger models is somewhat limited.
    *   **Specificity of Tasks:** The "even pairs" and "parity check" problems, while fundamental, are still relatively simple and abstract. Extending the analysis to more realistic language tasks would increase the impact.  However, their simplicity makes them analytically tractable.
    *   **Limited Real-world Validation:** Experiments on "shakespeare" and similar datasets would significantly enhance the paper's appeal. The reliance on synthetic data leaves a gap.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research by:
    *   Providing a framework for analyzing the training dynamics of transformers.
    *   Inspiring new architectural designs and training strategies based on the insights gained.
    *   Guiding empirical studies by suggesting specific aspects of transformer behavior to investigate.

*   **Score Justification:**

    While the paper makes several novel contributions, the simplicity of the model and the specificity of tasks limit its overall impact. The analysis techniques, however, are valuable and could be applied to more complex scenarios in future work. The key contribution is the inference side trick that unlocks parity check on an even pair transformer.

**Score: 7**

The paper offers solid theoretical insights and uses novel techniques, which are well verified with strong experimental validation. The use of tractable toy problems rather than complex datasets keeps it from being an 8.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Compact Recurrent Transformer with Persistent Memory
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.00929v1)
- **Authors**: Edison Mucllari, Zachary Daniels, David Zhang, Qiang Ye
- **Abstract**: The Transformer architecture has shown significant success in many language processing and visual tasks. However, the method faces challenges in efficiently scaling to long sequences because the self-attention computation is quadratic with respect to the input length. To overcome this limitation, several approaches scale to longer sequences by breaking long sequences into a series of segments, restricting self-attention to local dependencies between tokens within each segment and using a memory mechanism to manage information flow between segments. However, these approached generally introduce additional compute overhead that restricts them from being used for applications where limited compute memory and power are of great concern (such as edge computing). We propose a novel and efficient Compact Recurrent Transformer (CRT), which combines shallow Transformer models that process short local segments with recurrent neural networks to compress and manage a single persistent memory vector that summarizes long-range global information between segments. We evaluate CRT on WordPTB and WikiText-103 for next-token-prediction tasks, as well as on the Toyota Smarthome video dataset for classification. CRT achieves comparable or superior prediction results to full-length Transformers in the language datasets while using significantly shorter segments (half or quarter size) and substantially reduced FLOPs. Our approach also demonstrates state-of-the-art performance on the Toyota Smarthome video dataset.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a "Compact Recurrent Transformer" (CRT) architecture designed to address the quadratic complexity challenge of standard Transformers when applied to long sequences. CRT combines shallow Transformer models for local segment processing with recurrent neural networks (RNNs) to compress and manage a single, persistent memory vector. This memory vector summarizes long-range global information between segments. The authors evaluate CRT on next-token prediction tasks (WordPTB, WikiText-103) and video classification (Toyota Smarthome). Results indicate that CRT achieves comparable or superior performance to full-length Transformers and Transformer-XL on language datasets while utilizing shorter segments and reducing FLOPs. It also demonstrates state-of-the-art performance on the Toyota Smarthome video dataset. The key advantage is the memory compression and the potential for deployment on resource-constrained devices.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of combining shallow transformers with RNN-based memory compression is reasonably novel. The use of a single persistent memory *vector*, instead of blocks of memory tokens employed by existing approaches, does provide a tangible improvement on existing memory transformer architectures. The approach of using another RNN for positional encoding further leverages recurrence in the model.
*   **Significance:**  The paper's significance hinges on its efficiency.  The ability to achieve good performance with reduced FLOPs and memory requirements makes Transformer architectures more accessible to resource-constrained platforms. The potential deployment on edge devices or low-SWaP systems is a real-world consideration. The improved results on Toyota Smarthome also contribute positively. The authors rigorously compare the CRT to Transformer and Transformer-XL, which provide a solid footing in the evaluation. The ablation studies are also commendable in identifying and solidifying the effects of each component.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Performance:** The paper demonstrates solid empirical results on several benchmarks, showing either comparable or superior performance compared to standard Transformers and Transformer-XL, at a decreased computational cost.
    *   **Efficiency:**  The focus on FLOPs reduction and memory compression directly addresses the limitations of Transformers on long sequences and resource-limited devices.
    *   **Ablation studies:** Clear effort is made to isolate and quantify the effects of individual components of the network.
    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly states the problem of quadratic complexity and the need for efficiency.
    *   **Multi-domain Evaluation:** Evaluating across language and video datasets demonstrates generality.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Recurrence Overhead:** The paper acknowledges that RNNs introduce serial processing but argues that this is negligible given the focus on compact models and short segments. This needs to be carefully justified for real-world applications. The authors don't fully explore the limitations of the serial component, nor do they show how to overcome this limitation.
    *   **Incremental Improvement:** While the performance gains are reported, the overall improvement over highly optimized Transformer variants may be incremental in some settings, as the model architecture still draws heavily from existing work. The authors compare the CRT to well-known architectures in literature and highlight several benefits of the CRT.
    *   **Limited Scope of RNN Exploration:** The use of simple GRU/NCGRU might limit the model's capacity for more complex memory management strategies.

*   **Impact:**  If CRT proves to be widely applicable and easily deployable on edge devices, it could have a substantial impact by enabling more intelligent and efficient AI systems.  The work offers insights into efficient memory management for Transformers.

**Justification for Score:**

The CRT architecture provides a practical solution for making Transformers more efficient without significantly compromising performance. While it builds upon existing ideas from recurrent and memory-augmented Transformers, the specific combination of shallow Transformers, RNN-based memory *vector* compression, and RNN-based positional encoding is novel. The significance lies primarily in the potential for broader deployment on resource-constrained platforms. Although the performance gains compared to highly optimized Transformer variants may be incremental in certain scenarios, the FLOPs reduction and memory compression are notable.  The limitations include the serial overhead of RNNs and the relatively simple RNN architectures used. I would expect future investigations of CRT-like models to focus on the tradeoffs between different memory structures and the corresponding computational costs. I think a score of 7 reflects the paper's contributions to practical efficiency in transformer architectures.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Large Language Model-Driven Dynamic Assessment of Grammatical Accuracy in English Language Learner Writing
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.00931v1)
- **Authors**: Timur Jaganov, John Blake, Julián Villegas, Nicholas Carr
- **Abstract**: This study investigates the potential for Large Language Models (LLMs) to scale-up Dynamic Assessment (DA). To facilitate such an investigation, we first developed DynaWrite-a modular, microservices-based grammatical tutoring application which supports multiple LLMs to generate dynamic feedback to learners of English. Initial testing of 21 LLMs, revealed GPT-4o and neural chat to have the most potential to scale-up DA in the language learning classroom. Further testing of these two candidates found both models performed similarly in their ability to accurately identify grammatical errors in user sentences. However, GPT-4o consistently outperformed neural chat in the quality of its DA by generating clear, consistent, and progressively explicit hints. Real-time responsiveness and system stability were also confirmed through detailed performance testing, with GPT-4o exhibiting sufficient speed and stability. This study shows that LLMs can be used to scale-up dynamic assessment and thus enable dynamic assessment to be delivered to larger groups than possible in traditional teacher-learner settings.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper explores the potential of Large Language Models (LLMs) to enhance and scale up Dynamic Assessment (DA) for grammatical accuracy in English language learner writing. The authors developed DynaWrite, a modular web application that integrates multiple LLMs to provide dynamic, graduated feedback to learners. The study tested 21 LLMs and found GPT-4o and Neural Chat to be promising candidates. Further testing revealed that both models could accurately identify grammatical errors, but GPT-4o consistently outperformed Neural Chat in the quality of its DA, providing clearer, more consistent, and progressively explicit hints.  The system's real-time responsiveness and stability were also confirmed. The authors conclude that LLMs can be used to scale DA, enabling its delivery to larger groups of learners than previously possible.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Relevant and timely research:** The study addresses a significant challenge in language education – the difficulty of scaling dynamic assessment. Exploring the use of LLMs to automate and personalize this process is highly relevant in the current era of AI-driven education.
    *   **System Development:** The development of DynaWrite is a significant contribution. It provides a platform for testing and evaluating the effectiveness of LLMs for dynamic assessment, and this is a valuable resource for future research. The microservices architecture ensures scalability and flexibility.
    *   **Rigorous Evaluation:** The study employs a mixed-methods approach, combining quantitative metrics (accuracy, response time) with qualitative analysis of feedback quality (consistency, gradation, resolution). This comprehensive evaluation provides a well-rounded picture of the LLMs' performance.
    *   **Clear Presentation:** The paper is well-structured, clearly written, and includes helpful figures and tables to illustrate the findings.
    *   **Addresses a Niche:** The work specifically targets the niche of dynamic feedback, differentiating itself from general automated writing evaluation (AWE) tools.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Scope of Evaluation:** Although 21 models were initially tested, the in-depth analysis focuses primarily on two LLMs (GPT-4o and Neural Chat). While the initial screening justifies this, it limits the generalizability of the findings to other LLMs. The limited number of test sentences in the first evaluation also reduces the rigor of the comparison of all 21 models.
    *   **Lack of Human Subject Testing/Ecological Validity:** The key limitation is the lack of human subject testing. The evaluation of feedback quality is based on researcher judgment. The paper doesn't demonstrate whether learners actually *benefit* from the feedback in terms of improved writing skills or learning outcomes. The evaluation lacks ecological validity, i.e. the degree to which the results can be applied to real-world language learning contexts.
    *   **Subjectivity in Feedback Evaluation:** While the criteria for evaluating feedback (consistency, gradation, resolution) are defined, the assessment of whether a particular LLM response meets these criteria is inherently subjective. The researchers could have increased the evaluation's reliability by having multiple raters assess the feedback independently and then compare their ratings.
    *   **Limited Consideration of Error Types:** While the study focuses on grammatical accuracy, other aspects of writing quality (e.g., vocabulary, style, organization) are not considered.
    *   **Unclear Justification for the Selection of LLMs:** The criteria used for selecting LLMs could be more transparent. Mentioning the size constraint but not elaborating more thoroughly on the choices hinders reproducibility.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**

    *   The application of LLMs to dynamic assessment in language learning is novel.  While AWE tools are common, this study is one of the first to explore the potential of LLMs to provide graduated, context-sensitive feedback in a dynamic assessment framework.
    *   The creation of DynaWrite as a platform for LLM-driven DA is a significant contribution, offering a valuable tool for researchers in the field.
    *   The findings provide valuable insights into the capabilities and limitations of different LLMs for dynamic assessment, guiding future research and development in this area.

**Justification for Score:**

Despite its strengths, the lack of user testing significantly limits the paper's impact. Without demonstrating that the LLM-generated feedback actually improves learner outcomes, the study remains primarily a technical evaluation of LLM performance. Also, as the article acknowledges in its 'Limitations' section, the system's current coverage of available LLMs is not comprehensive. I'm giving it a score of 6.

**Score: 6**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### Seeking to Collide: Online Safety-Critical Scenario Generation for Autonomous Driving with Retrieval Augmented Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.00972v1)
- **Authors**: Yuewen Mei, Tong Nie, Jian Sun, Ye Tian
- **Abstract**: Simulation-based testing is crucial for validating autonomous vehicles (AVs), yet existing scenario generation methods either overfit to common driving patterns or operate in an offline, non-interactive manner that fails to expose rare, safety-critical corner cases. In this paper, we introduce an online, retrieval-augmented large language model (LLM) framework for generating safety-critical driving scenarios. Our method first employs an LLM-based behavior analyzer to infer the most dangerous intent of the background vehicle from the observed state, then queries additional LLM agents to synthesize feasible adversarial trajectories. To mitigate catastrophic forgetting and accelerate adaptation, we augment the framework with a dynamic memorization and retrieval bank of intent-planner pairs, automatically expanding its behavioral library when novel intents arise. Evaluations using the Waymo Open Motion Dataset demonstrate that our model reduces the mean minimum time-to-collision from 1.62 to 1.08 s and incurs a 75% collision rate, substantially outperforming baselines.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel online, retrieval-augmented large language model (LLM) framework for generating safety-critical driving scenarios. The framework utilizes an LLM-based behavior analyzer to infer the dangerous intent of background vehicles from observed states, then prompts other LLM agents to synthesize adversarial trajectories. A dynamic memorization and retrieval bank of intent-planner pairs is used to mitigate catastrophic forgetting and accelerate adaptation when novel intents arise. Experiments on the Waymo Open Motion Dataset demonstrate significant improvement in reducing the mean minimum time-to-collision (TTC) and increasing collision rates compared to baselines.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the online and interactive integration of LLMs for safety-critical scenario generation. Existing methods often operate offline or rely on pre-trained adversarial priors. The use of retrieval-augmented generation and a dynamic memory mechanism to adapt LLMs to changing scenarios and overcome catastrophic forgetting in this specific context is a solid contribution. While LLMs have been used for scenario generation before, their application in a dynamically adapting, closed-loop system specifically for identifying and generating safety-critical situations is a significant step forward. The idea of building a behavior library online is also a unique aspect.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a critical challenge in autonomous vehicle validation: the generation of rare, safety-critical scenarios that are not adequately covered by traditional data-driven approaches. By explicitly targeting dangerous intents and generating adversarial trajectories, the proposed framework has the potential to improve the robustness and safety of AVs. The fact that they show results on Waymo dataset is a plus. The performance improvements over baselines are significant, and the framework's adaptability to novel scenarios is a key advantage.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear problem definition and motivation.
    *   Well-defined framework with modular components.
    *   Novel integration of LLMs, retrieval-augmented generation, and a dynamic memory mechanism.
    *   Significant performance improvements compared to baselines.
    *   Demonstrated adaptability to novel scenarios.
    *   Use of a real-world dataset for validation.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The paper doesn't clearly address the computational cost of repeatedly prompting LLMs in an online setting. The efficiency of the method is a crucial practical consideration.
    *   Limited discussion about the robustness of the LLM-based behavior analyzer. How sensitive is it to noise or uncertainty in the observed states?
    *   The reliance on LLM APIs introduces a dependency that might affect reproducibility and accessibility.
    *   While the authors demonstrate the approach generates more dangerous scenarios than the baselines, more details could be provided about specific types of scenarios that it finds, and scenarios that the baselines miss.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to influence future research in safety-critical scenario generation for AVs. It provides a compelling demonstration of the potential of LLMs for adaptive and interactive scenario generation. The proposed framework could serve as a foundation for developing more sophisticated and efficient methods for adversarial testing of AVs. The online adaptation aspect will likely be a central theme in future work.

**Score:** 7.5

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a novel and significant contribution to safety-critical scenario generation by effectively leveraging LLMs in an online, adaptive manner. The retrieval-augmented approach addresses the limitations of existing methods and shows strong performance improvements. However, the paper's practical limitations (computational cost, reliance on LLM APIs) and could benefit from further analysis (analyzer robustness and more scenario-specific analysis) prevent it from achieving a higher score. The overall impact and promise of the approach is clear, making it a valuable contribution to the field.
Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### Attack and defense techniques in large language models: A survey and new perspectives
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.00976v1)
- **Authors**: Zhiyu Liao, Kang Chen, Yuanguo Lin, Kangkang Li, Yunxuan Liu, Hefeng Chen, Xingwang Huang, Yuanhui Yu
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) have become central to numerous natural language processing tasks, but their vulnerabilities present significant security and ethical challenges. This systematic survey explores the evolving landscape of attack and defense techniques in LLMs. We classify attacks into adversarial prompt attack, optimized attacks, model theft, as well as attacks on application of LLMs, detailing their mechanisms and implications. Consequently, we analyze defense strategies, including prevention-based and detection-based defense methods. Although advances have been made, challenges remain to adapt to the dynamic threat landscape, balance usability with robustness, and address resource constraints in defense implementation. We highlight open problems, including the need for adaptive scalable defenses, explainable security techniques, and standardized evaluation frameworks. This survey provides actionable insights and directions for developing secure and resilient LLMs, emphasizing the importance of interdisciplinary collaboration and ethical considerations to mitigate risks in real-world applications.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided survey paper:

**Summary:**

The paper presents a survey of attack and defense techniques for large language models (LLMs). It classifies attacks into adversarial prompt attacks, optimized attacks, model theft, and attacks on LLM applications. Defenses are categorized as prevention-based or detection-based. The survey also discusses practical challenges like resistance to evasion attacks, robustness against poisoning, adversarial attack detection, lack of generalized defenses, and ethical concerns. The paper highlights open problems, including adaptive defenses, explainable security, and standardized evaluation frameworks, aiming to guide future research towards building more secure and resilient LLMs.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Comprehensive Coverage:** The paper provides a relatively broad overview of the attack and defense landscape in LLM security. It covers the major attack vectors and a decent selection of corresponding defense strategies. The classifications into prevention-based and detection-based strategies is helpful for organizing the field.
    *   **Clear Taxonomy:** The hierarchical taxonomy of attacks and defenses (summarized in Figure 1) offers a clear structure for understanding the various approaches and their interrelationships.
    *   **Practical Challenges:** The inclusion of a section on practical challenges highlights the difficulties faced in deploying and maintaining secure LLMs in real-world settings. This section adds value by pointing to crucial areas needing further research.
    *   **Focus on Applications:**  The specific attention paid to attacks on LLM-integrated applications is a welcome addition, as this is a growing area of vulnerability often overlooked.
    *   **Well-Referenced:** The paper cites a large number of recent and relevant publications, which is a good indicator that the authors are familiar with the state-of-the-art.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Depth of Analysis:** While broad, the depth of analysis for each attack and defense technique is sometimes limited. The survey often summarizes the approach without deep dives into the underlying mechanisms or providing critical comparative analyses between different approaches.  The "how" and "why" are sometimes skimmed over, focusing more on "what."
    *   **Novelty of Insights:** The survey, while comprehensive, primarily aggregates existing knowledge. There's a lack of truly novel insights or perspectives that significantly shift the understanding of LLM security. It primarily categorizes known approaches, rather than offering a new framework for thinking about the problem.
    *   **Evaluation Criteria:** The paper acknowledges the lack of standardized evaluation frameworks but doesn't propose concrete solutions or specific methodologies for addressing this problem.  This is a missed opportunity to contribute more actively to shaping the field.
    *   **Limited Critical Assessment:**  The paper is largely descriptive rather than critically evaluative. A stronger paper would include more comparisons between techniques and critiques of the existing research, identifying gaps and limitations in existing solutions more thoroughly.
    *   **Focus on Specific Areas:** Although comprehensive, more emphasis is placed on specific areas like jailbreak attacks, suggesting a possible bias or limitation in the authors' expertise.

*   **Significance:**

    *   **Educational Resource:** The paper serves as a useful educational resource for newcomers to the field of LLM security. The taxonomy and overview of techniques can help researchers and practitioners get a handle on the vast and rapidly evolving literature.
    *   **Identifies Key Challenges:** The discussion of practical challenges highlights important areas where further research is needed, potentially influencing the direction of future work in the field.
    *   **Brings Attention to Application Security:** Emphasizing the vulnerabilities in LLM-based applications can spur more research and development in securing these deployed systems.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper makes a decent contribution as an educational resource and a guide to the existing literature. However, it lacks significant novelty and critical assessment. The breadth of coverage comes at the expense of depth in analysis and critical evaluation.  While the identified challenges are important, the paper doesn't offer concrete or original strategies for addressing them. A truly impactful survey would not only categorize existing work but also synthesize it to create new insights and strongly influence future research directions. It serves more as a catalog than a catalyst.

**Score: 6**

- **Classification**: cs.CR
- **Score**: 6/10

### Synthesize-on-Graph: Knowledgeable Synthetic Data Generation for Continue Pre-training of Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.00979v1)
- **Authors**: Xuhui Jiang, Shengjie Ma, Chengjin Xu, Cehao Yang, Liyu Zhang, Jian Guo
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) have achieved remarkable success but remain data-inefficient, especially when learning from small, specialized corpora with limited and proprietary data. Existing synthetic data generation methods for continue pre-training focus on intra-document content and overlook cross-document knowledge associations, limiting content diversity and depth. We propose Synthetic-on-Graph (SoG), a synthetic data generation framework that incorporates cross-document knowledge associations for efficient corpus expansion. SoG constructs a context graph by extracting entities and concepts from the original corpus, representing cross-document associations, and employing a graph walk strategy for knowledge-associated sampling. This enhances synthetic data diversity and coherence, enabling models to learn complex knowledge structures and handle rare knowledge. To further improve synthetic data quality, we integrate Chain-of-Thought (CoT) and Contrastive Clarifying (CC) synthetic, enhancing reasoning processes and discriminative power. Experiments show that SoG outperforms the state-of-the-art (SOTA) method in a multi-hop document Q&A dataset while performing comparably to the SOTA method on the reading comprehension task datasets, which also underscores the better generalization capability of SoG. Our work advances synthetic data generation and provides practical solutions for efficient knowledge acquisition in LLMs, especially in domains with limited data availability.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Synthetic-on-Graph (SoG), a novel framework for synthetic data generation designed to improve the continue pre-training (CPT) of Large Language Models (LLMs), especially when dealing with small or limited domain-specific datasets. SoG addresses the limitations of existing methods that primarily focus on intra-document content by incorporating cross-document knowledge associations.  It constructs a context graph by extracting entities and concepts from the original corpus and represents cross-document relationships. A graph walk strategy facilitates knowledge-associated sampling to enhance data diversity and coherence. The framework further improves data quality by integrating Chain-of-Thought (CoT) and Contrastive Clarifying (CC) synthetic approaches, promoting reasoning abilities and discriminative power in the generated data. Experiments on a multi-hop document question-answering dataset show that SoG outperforms state-of-the-art (SOTA) methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**  The paper's primary novelty lies in its systematic integration of cross-document knowledge within a graph-based synthetic data generation framework. While synthetic data generation and graph-based methods are not entirely new concepts, their combination to specifically address the limitations of existing synthetic data methods for CPT, especially for low-resource domains, demonstrates a significant contribution. The integration of CoT and CC synthetic methods to enhance data quality is another value-added that improves reasoning processes and discriminative power.

**Significance:** The research is significant because it tackles a critical bottleneck in LLM training: the data inefficiency problem, which becomes especially acute in specialized domains with limited and/or proprietary data. By enabling models to learn from richer, more diverse synthetic data that incorporates cross-document knowledge, SoG can lead to better performance in specialized domains, thereby expanding the applicability of LLMs. Demonstrating that SoG can surpass SOTA results in a multi-hop Q&A setting underscores its potential for enhancing LLM capabilities in scenarios requiring complex reasoning and information synthesis.

**Strengths:**

*   **Problem Definition:** Clearly articulates the limitations of existing synthetic data generation methods, emphasizing the lack of cross-document knowledge integration.
*   **Methodological Innovation:** The SoG framework offers a well-defined process, from context graph construction to cross-document sampling and synthetic data generation with CoT and CC.
*   **Empirical Validation:**  The paper presents experimental results demonstrating that SoG outperforms SOTA methods on a challenging multi-hop document Q&A dataset.
*   **Ablation Study:** Includes an ablation study that examines different generation strategies, providing insights into the impact of various components of the SoG framework.
*   **CPT vs. RAG comparison**: The comparison of using CPT vs. RAG for knowledge retrieval provides more real-world applications of how the results translate.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Generalization Concerns:** While SoG exhibits superior performance on the MHRAG dataset, its performance on the QUALITY dataset, where cross-document knowledge is less relevant, is only comparable to SOTA methods. This suggests that SoG's advantage might be less pronounced for tasks that do not heavily rely on knowledge integration.
*   **Dataset Dependence:** The effectiveness of the method may vary across different knowledge domains and datasets, requiring tuning of parameters.
*   **Engineering Details:** The specific details of how to adapt/tune the SoG framework to certain tasks need to be defined more precisely to address the concerns of different configurations across datasets in section 4.6.
*   **Scalability Considerations:** Constructing a context graph for very large corpora could be computationally expensive. The paper does not thoroughly discuss the scalability aspects of SoG.
*   **Limitations section:** A more comprehensive set of limitations would be helpful for better understanding the applications of the research.

**Potential Influence:**  SoG has the potential to influence research directions in synthetic data generation, particularly in the context of CPT for LLMs. It provides a blueprint for incorporating structured knowledge and reasoning into the data generation process. If the scalability concerns can be addressed, SoG could be adopted as a practical solution for training LLMs in domains where data is scarce or proprietary.

**Score: 7.5**

**Rationale:** The paper introduces a novel and promising approach to synthetic data generation that addresses a crucial problem in LLM training: data inefficiency in low-resource domains. The empirical results are compelling, demonstrating superior performance on a multi-hop Q&A task. However, some of its applicability across various domains needs to be more precisely addressed to improve future application and tuning. Overall, the paper demonstrates a significant contribution.
- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Multi-agents based User Values Mining for Recommendation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.00981v1)
- **Authors**: Lijian Chen, Wei Yuan, Tong Chen, Xiangyu Zhao, Nguyen Quoc Viet Hung, Hongzhi Yin
- **Abstract**: Recommender systems have rapidly evolved and become integral to many online services. However, existing systems sometimes produce unstable and unsatisfactory recommendations that fail to align with users' fundamental and long-term preferences. This is because they primarily focus on extracting shallow and short-term interests from user behavior data, which is inherently dynamic and challenging to model. Unlike these transient interests, user values are more stable and play a crucial role in shaping user behaviors, such as purchasing items and consuming content. Incorporating user values into recommender systems can help stabilize recommendation performance and ensure results better reflect users' latent preferences. However, acquiring user values is typically difficult and costly. To address this challenge, we leverage the strong language understanding, zero-shot inference, and generalization capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs) to extract user values from users' historical interactions. Unfortunately, direct extraction using LLMs presents several challenges such as length constraints and hallucination. To overcome these issues, we propose ZOOM, a zero-shot multi-LLM collaborative framework for effective and accurate user value extraction. In ZOOM, we apply text summarization techniques to condense item content while preserving essential meaning. To mitigate hallucinations, ZOOM introduces two specialized agent roles: evaluators and supervisors, to collaboratively generate accurate user values. Extensive experiments on two widely used recommendation datasets with two state-of-the-art recommendation models demonstrate the effectiveness and generalization of our framework in automatic user value mining and recommendation performance improvement.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Multi-agents based User Values Mining for Recommendation" proposes a novel framework called ZOOM for automatically extracting user values from historical interactions to improve recommender system performance. ZOOM leverages Large Language Models (LLMs) in a multi-agent collaborative setup, utilizing evaluators and supervisors to mitigate LLM input length limitations and hallucinations. Evaluators summarize item content and generate candidate user values, while supervisors refine these values through a debate process. The extracted user values are then incorporated into recommendation models using direct concatenation and a contrastive learning approach. The paper presents extensive experiments on two datasets using state-of-the-art recommendation models, demonstrating the effectiveness and generalization of the framework in user value mining and recommendation performance improvement.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper introduces a novel approach to user value mining. While LLMs have been used in recommendation systems before, this paper's multi-agent collaborative framework for user value extraction is a unique contribution. The idea of employing evaluators and supervisors to reduce hallucinations and input limitations is innovative. The fusion of user values with recommendation models via contrastive learning further adds to the novelty. The novelty also stems from the explicit focus on user values, which is a less explored area compared to traditional interest modeling.

*   **Significance:** Incorporating user values into recommender systems has the potential to address the limitations of current systems that often rely on transient interests. The ability to automatically extract these values significantly improves the feasibility of value-aware recommendation. The results showing performance improvements in various scenarios (with MoRec and EasyRec), especially in cases where direct concatenation fails, underscores the practical significance of the proposed contrastive learning approach. The paper also addresses a critical limitation of LLMs: hallucinations in complex tasks.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Well-defined Problem and Solution:** The paper clearly identifies the problem (unstable recommendations due to transient interests) and proposes a plausible solution (incorporating user values) with a technically sound framework (ZOOM).
    *   **Technically Sound:** The paper combines techniques from various areas (LLMs, summarization, multi-agent systems, contrastive learning) in a coherent and effective way.
    *   **Extensive Experiments:** The evaluation is thorough, with experiments on two datasets, two baseline models, and ablation studies to demonstrate the effectiveness of individual components.
    *   **Detailed Analysis:** The paper provides insightful analysis of the experimental results, explaining the observed performance gains and differences between the proposed methods.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Complexity:** The ZOOM framework is somewhat complex, involving multiple LLMs and agents. This might make it more challenging to implement and deploy in practice compared to simpler approaches.
    *   **Computational Cost:** The paper does not explicitly discuss the computational cost associated with running multiple LLMs for value extraction. It is likely to be more expensive than traditional recommendation approaches.
    *   **Dependency on LLM Performance:** The success of ZOOM is heavily dependent on the performance of the underlying LLMs. If the LLMs fail to understand the user's interactions or generate meaningful summaries, the entire framework may suffer.
    *   **Lack of qualitative evaluation of extracted user values.** While the paper has quantitative evaluations, it would be valuable to provide a more in-depth qualitative assessment to support the improvements to the general LLM extraction performance that ZOON provides.

*   **Potential Impact:** The paper has the potential to influence research in the area of personalized recommendation. It highlights the importance of considering user values and provides a practical approach to incorporating them into recommender systems. The multi-agent collaboration framework can also inspire new approaches to address the limitations of LLMs in other complex tasks. The demonstration of significant improvements to two important baselines will motivate researchers to use and extend this work.

**Rigorous Rationale for Assigned Score:**

The paper presents a significant advancement in the field of recommender systems by successfully incorporating user values through a novel and well-engineered multi-agent LLM-based framework. The empirical results are solid, with clear improvements observed across various scenarios. While there are limitations related to computational cost and complexity, the potential benefits in terms of recommendation stability and personalization outweigh these drawbacks. Also, the limited discussion on the computational aspect has not been sufficiently discussed. The authors provide substantial discussion, ablation studies, and analysis of their results, which significantly strengthen the validity of their claims. The novelty and potential impact of the work justify a score in the upper range.

**Score: 8**

- **Classification**: cs.IR
- **Score**: 8/10

### Position: Enough of Scaling LLMs! Lets Focus on Downscaling
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.00985v1)
- **Authors**: Ayan Sengupta, Yash Goel, Tanmoy Chakraborty
- **Abstract**: We challenge the dominant focus on neural scaling laws and advocate for a paradigm shift toward downscaling in the development of large language models (LLMs). While scaling laws have provided critical insights into performance improvements through increasing model and dataset size, we emphasize the significant limitations of this approach, particularly in terms of computational inefficiency, environmental impact, and deployment constraints. To address these challenges, we propose a holistic framework for downscaling LLMs that seeks to maintain performance while drastically reducing resource demands. This paper outlines practical strategies for transitioning away from traditional scaling paradigms, advocating for a more sustainable, efficient, and accessible approach to LLM development.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper abstract and partial paper content.

**Summary:**

The paper challenges the prevailing focus on neural scaling laws (increasing model size, dataset size, and compute) in large language model (LLM) development. It argues for a shift towards downscaling, citing concerns about computational inefficiency, environmental impact, and deployment limitations. The paper proposes a holistic framework for downscaling LLMs while maintaining performance, suggesting practical strategies for transitioning to a more sustainable, efficient, and accessible approach to LLM development.  The document discusses existing scaling laws, critiques them, examines the rise of smaller, efficient language models (SLMs), and presents concrete recommendations including dataset optimization, model downscaling, and ensembling techniques, culminating in a proposed downscaling pipeline. It ends by addressing limitations of downscaling and ensembling and advocating for careful consideration in implementation.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**  The paper's novelty is moderate. While the *idea* of shifting focus to downscaling is not entirely new (researchers have been exploring efficiency for a while), the explicit framing as a *challenge* to the dominant scaling laws paradigm, coupled with the proposed holistic framework, adds a layer of contribution.  The detailed analysis of various strategies like data pruning, model compression, and ensembling, and their integration into a pipeline, demonstrates a practical approach. The discussion of SLMs and specific examples contributes to supporting the case for downscaling. However, some of these individual techniques (data pruning, distillation) are well-established. The value lies in the combination and the articulation of the overall philosophy.

**Significance:** The significance lies in the potential to redirect research efforts towards more sustainable and accessible AI. If the proposed framework proves effective, it could significantly reduce the resource demands of LLMs, enabling wider adoption and reducing the environmental footprint. However, the *actual* impact depends on the concrete results obtained from implementing the proposed pipeline and demonstrating its effectiveness compared to purely scaling-based approaches.  The inclusion of the CO2 footprint analysis, albeit simplified, is valuable for emphasizing the environmental dimension. The claim that the presented models should be useful for edge implementations highlights the real-world applicability of the research direction. The critique of existing scaling laws, pinpointing their limitations, is important for guiding future research.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear articulation of the problem:**  The paper clearly identifies the limitations of the "bigger is always better" approach.
*   **Holistic Framework:** It proposes a cohesive framework encompassing various downscaling techniques.
*   **Practical focus:** The emphasis on practical strategies and concrete examples (SLMs, D-CPT law, specific pruning techniques) makes the paper more actionable.
*   **Environmental awareness:**  The inclusion of carbon emission considerations is a significant strength.
*   **Comprehensive review of relevant literature:** Demonstrates deep understanding of current trends

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Lack of empirical validation:** The abstract and partial paper (judging from what is provided) primarily focus on proposing and justifying the framework. Crucially, *empirical validation* of the pipeline's effectiveness is missing.  Without experimental results demonstrating superior performance or resource efficiency compared to scaling-based approaches, the paper's impact is limited.
*   **Simplified CO2 Analysis:** While commendable, the CO2 analysis could benefit from more detailed modeling of various hardware configurations and energy consumption patterns.
*   **Dependence on existing techniques:** The pipeline relies on established techniques (data pruning, knowledge distillation, ensembling). The novelty resides in their integration rather than fundamental breakthroughs.
*   **Limitations acknowledged only at the very end:** By including limitations only towards the end, it is very easy for someone to gloss over them and come away with the impression that the paper offers a one-size-fits-all solution.

**Justification for the Score:**

I assign a score of **6**.

Here's the rationale: The paper provides a valuable perspective shift and a structured approach to downscaling LLMs, addressing important concerns about sustainability and accessibility. It presents a well-articulated critique of current scaling laws and proposes a cohesive framework built on existing techniques. This represents a moderately significant contribution by providing a practical roadmap.

However, the *absence of empirical validation* is a major weakness. The paper presents a theoretical framework and a pipeline, but the lack of experimental results demonstrating the effectiveness of the proposed approach prevents a higher score. Without demonstrating that the downscaling pipeline *actually works* and *outperforms scaling-based alternatives* in terms of resource efficiency and/or environmental impact, the paper's impact remains largely speculative. A rigorous comparison, showing measurable improvements, would significantly elevate the paper's contribution. If experimental results are demonstrated, a score of 7 or 8 would be justified. Therefore, a score of 6 reflects the potential value and direction provided by the paper, tempered by the need for empirical confirmation of its claims.
Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### Togedule: Scheduling Meetings with Large Language Models and Adaptive Representations of Group Availability
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.01000v1)
- **Authors**: Jaeyoon Song, Zahra Ashktorab, Thomas W. Malone
- **Abstract**: Scheduling is a perennial-and often challenging-problem for many groups. Existing tools are mostly static, showing an identical set of choices to everyone, regardless of the current status of attendees' inputs and preferences. In this paper, we propose Togedule, an adaptive scheduling tool that uses large language models to dynamically adjust the pool of choices and their presentation format. With the initial prototype, we conducted a formative study (N=10) and identified the potential benefits and risks of such an adaptive scheduling tool. Then, after enhancing the system, we conducted two controlled experiments, one each for attendees and organizers (total N=66). For each experiment, we compared scheduling with verbal messages, shared calendars, or Togedule. Results show that Togedule significantly reduces the cognitive load of attendees indicating their availability and improves the speed and quality of the decisions made by organizers.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "Togedule," an adaptive scheduling tool that leverages large language models (LLMs) to streamline the process of scheduling meetings. Togedule dynamically adjusts the pool of available time slots and their presentation format based on attendees' inputs, preferences, and priorities, aiming to reduce cognitive load and improve decision-making. The system switches between a shared calendar and a poll view dynamically, leveraging LLMs to determine the optimal presentation for each attendee. The authors conducted a formative study and two controlled experiments to evaluate Togedule's effectiveness, comparing it to traditional methods like verbal communication and shared calendars. Results show that Togedule reduces cognitive load for attendees, decreases the need for verbal communication, and enhances the speed and quality of organizers' decisions.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the *adaptive* representation of group availability using LLMs for dynamic adjustment of scheduling options. Most existing tools are static, presenting the same information to all users regardless of individual preferences. While AI-powered scheduling assistants exist, they often focus on full automation, whereas Togedule specifically enhances the voting/selection stage within a hybrid human-AI collaborative framework. The idea of dynamically shifting between calendar and poll views based on participant interaction data is also a notable aspect. However, the individual components -- shared calendars, polls, and LLMs -- are not new. The integration and dynamic application are the key innovations.

*   **Significance:** The potential significance of Togedule lies in its ability to address a persistent problem: the time-consuming and often frustrating process of scheduling meetings. By reducing cognitive load and enhancing decision quality, Togedule could improve productivity and satisfaction in collaborative settings. The finding that Togedule significantly reduced verbal communication required, compared to traditional methods, suggests a practical and impactful improvement over existing practices. It also offers insights into how AI can augment human-centered collaborative decision making.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Well-structured evaluation:** The paper uses a mixed-methods approach with a formative study followed by controlled experiments. This allows for a comprehensive understanding of Togedule's benefits and limitations.

    *   **Clear problem definition:** The authors clearly articulate the challenges associated with traditional scheduling methods.

    *   **Practical implications:** The findings suggest that Togedule has the potential to improve real-world scheduling practices.

    *   **Detailed implementation:** The paper provides sufficient detail on Togedule's design and implementation, including the use of GPT-4 and the prompt engineering process.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Controlled experiment limitations:** The controlled experiment, while rigorous, might not fully capture the complexities of real-world scheduling scenarios. The incentive structure and artificial calendars may influence user behavior.

    *   **LLM Explainability:** The "black box" nature of LLMs' decision-making remains a concern. While the paper addresses this by retrieving reasons from GPT-4, the actual reasoning process is not fully transparent.

    *   **Generalizability:** The study was conducted with a specific set of participants (Prolific workers). It would be beneficial to evaluate Togedule with a more diverse user base.

    *   **Reliance on external API:** The use of the GPT-4 API brings the question of reproducibility with future GPT-4 models, as well as API cost issues that need to be considered if Togedule is to be actually deployed.

*   **Potential Impact:** The study's findings pave the way for future research into adaptive scheduling tools and highlight the potential of LLMs to augment human collaboration. The design principles and evaluation results provide valuable insights for developing similar systems in other domains.

**Justification of Score:**

Considering the factors above, Togedule presents a novel and significant contribution to the field. The combination of established technologies (calendars, polls, LLMs) in a dynamic and adaptive manner is unique and addresses a real-world problem. The experimental results support the effectiveness of Togedule in reducing cognitive load and improving decision-making. However, the limitations of the controlled experiment and the dependence on LLMs' explainability prevent a higher score.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.HC
- **Score**: 7/10

### 3D Human Pose Estimation via Spatial Graph Order Attention and Temporal Body Aware Transformer
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.01003v1)
- **Authors**: Kamel Aouaidjia, Aofan Li, Wenhao Zhang, Chongsheng Zhang
- **Abstract**: Nowadays, Transformers and Graph Convolutional Networks (GCNs) are the prevailing techniques for 3D human pose estimation. However, Transformer-based methods either ignore the spatial neighborhood relationships between the joints when used for skeleton representations or disregard the local temporal patterns of the local joint movements in skeleton sequence modeling, while GCN-based methods often neglect the need for pose-specific representations. To address these problems, we propose a new method that exploits the graph modeling capability of GCN to represent each skeleton with multiple graphs of different orders, incorporated with a newly introduced Graph Order Attention module that dynamically emphasizes the most representative orders for each joint. The resulting spatial features of the sequence are further processed using a proposed temporal Body Aware Transformer that models the global body feature dependencies in the sequence with awareness of the local inter-skeleton feature dependencies of joints. Given that our 3D pose output aligns with the central 2D pose in the sequence, we improve the self-attention mechanism to be aware of the central pose while diminishing its focus gradually towards the first and the last poses. Extensive experiments on Human3.6m, MPIINF-3DHP, and HumanEva-I datasets demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed method. Code and models are made available on Github.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel approach to 3D human pose estimation from a sequence of 2D poses.
