It tackles the limitations of existing methods that either ignore spatial relationships within a skeleton or disregard local temporal patterns in joint movements. The core of the approach is a combination of:

1.  **Multi-Order Graph Convolutional Networks (MO-GCN):**  It represents each skeleton using multiple graph orders to capture varying degrees of neighborhood connectivity between joints.
2.  **Graph Order Attention (GOA):** A module that dynamically emphasizes the most representative graph order for each joint, allowing for pose-specific representations and mitigating noise from less relevant orders.
3.  **Temporal Body Aware Transformer (BAT):** A temporal transformer enhanced with:
    *   **Joints Weighted Attention (JWA):** Models the temporal evolution of individual joints, focusing on the most informative frames.
    *   **Body-Centred Multi-Head Attention (BCMA):** Emphasizes global body features, focusing on the central frames of the sequence to align with the target 3D pose.

The approach is evaluated on Human3.6m, MPI-INF-3DHP, and HumanEva-I datasets, demonstrating improved performance compared to existing methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a good combination of existing techniques (GCNs and Transformers) with several novel modules that enhances its performance in 3D human pose estimation. The novelty lies mainly in the attention mechanisms it uses. GOA mechanism provides a weighted GCN for the various features, and the BAT is also new and more optimized than the classical Transformers in various manners like the attention modules(BCMA and JWA) or in mitigating overfitting. The improvements brought by these contributions are thoroughly validated in various experimental setups.
*   **Significance:** The paper addresses relevant limitations in the field of 3D human pose estimation by incorporating spatial and temporal awareness. It is capable of achieving good results in pose estimation tasks,
*   **Strengths:**
    *   The integration of GCNs and Transformers is well-motivated and addresses complementary limitations.
    *   The GOA module is a valuable contribution, enabling dynamic adaptation to different pose configurations.
    *   The BAT architecture, with JWA and BCMA, improves temporal modeling by focusing on relevant joint movements and emphasizing the central pose.
    *   The experimental results demonstrate consistent improvements across multiple datasets.
    *   The ablation study provides a good understanding of the contribution of each module.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   While the combination of techniques is novel, each individual component (GCN, Transformer, attention) is relatively well-established.  The contribution is primarily in the specific design and integration of these components.
    *   The paper describes a number of overfitting mitigation strategies suggesting the need for better regularization.
    *   Some of the qualitative failure cases are quite pronounced (e.g., Fig 9), highlighting the method's limitations in fast-motion scenarios.
    *   The increased complexity introduced by multiple modules, along with overfitting mitigation strategies, might make the approach computationally expensive, impacting the deployment in resource-constrained scenarios. This is partially addressed in the computation complexity analysis, but further practical considerations should be explored.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research in 3D human pose estimation, particularly in the development of more adaptive and context-aware architectures. The GOA and BAT modules offer valuable insights into how to effectively combine GCNs and Transformers for this task. The analysis of training data size and dropping rates could also be beneficial to other researchers.

**Score: 7.5**

**Justification:**

The paper presents a technically sound approach with several novel components that effectively address limitations in existing 3D human pose estimation methods. The experimental results demonstrate a clear improvement in performance. However, the novelty is incremental, building upon established techniques. The performance is solid, and it does offer interesting insights that could inform future research. The strengths justify a good score, but the incremental nature of the novelty and certain limitations prevent a higher score.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Towards the Resistance of Neural Network Watermarking to Fine-tuning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.01007v1)
- **Authors**: Ling Tang, Yuefeng Chen, Hui Xue, Quanshi Zhang
- **Abstract**: This paper proves a new watermarking method to embed the ownership information into a deep neural network (DNN), which is robust to fine-tuning. Specifically, we prove that when the input feature of a convolutional layer only contains low-frequency components, specific frequency components of the convolutional filter will not be changed by gradient descent during the fine-tuning process, where we propose a revised Fourier transform to extract frequency components from the convolutional filter. Additionally, we also prove that these frequency components are equivariant to weight scaling and weight permutations. In this way, we design a watermark module to encode the watermark information to specific frequency components in a convolutional filter. Preliminary experiments demonstrate the effectiveness of our method.
- **Summary**: Okay, I will provide a summary and critical evaluation of the paper, including a novelty and significance score, with a justification.

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel watermarking technique for deep neural networks (DNNs) designed to be robust against fine-tuning. The core idea revolves around leveraging the stability of specific frequency components within the convolutional filters of a DNN layer. The authors theoretically prove that if the input feature contains predominantly low-frequency components, specific frequency components of the convolutional filters remain relatively unchanged during fine-tuning. They also prove that these frequency components exhibit equivariance to weight scaling and weight permutations, common attack vectors against watermarks. Based on these theoretical findings, they propose a watermark module that encodes ownership information into these stable frequency components. The effectiveness of the proposed method is then evaluated through preliminary experiments.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Theoretical Foundation:** The paper's primary strength lies in its theoretical grounding. The authors provide mathematical proofs demonstrating the invariance of specific frequency components to fine-tuning under certain conditions (low-frequency input features). This theoretical underpinning differentiates it from many existing watermarking techniques that rely on empirical observations.
*   **Robustness Against Common Attacks:** The method is provably robust against weight scaling and weight permutation attacks, which are significant vulnerabilities for many watermark schemes.
*   **Clear Problem Statement:** The paper clearly identifies the challenge of watermark robustness to fine-tuning, a known limitation in the field.
*   **Well-Defined Approach:** The approach is well-defined, explaining the frequency components extraction method and the watermark embedding process.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Scope/Assumptions:** The theoretical results are based on a key assumption: the input feature must primarily contain low-frequency components. While the authors propose using a low-pass filter in their watermark module, the impact of this constraint on practical applications and performance (e.g., classification accuracy) needs to be carefully considered and thoroughly examined across different dataset types where natural images might not fit this description. The experiments should explore models trained *only* using the watermarked layers in a way that this limitation is explicitly accounted for (or removed).
*   **Empirical Validation:** The experimental evaluation is preliminary. While the initial results show promise, the method needs to be tested on a broader range of datasets, network architectures, and fine-tuning scenarios to assess its generalizability and robustness comprehensively. The scale of the networks that they're working on also limits their findings - the applicability of these methods to LLMs, as an example, isn't clear.
*   **Overwriting Attack Resistance:** The method introduces an additional loss function to mitigate overwriting attacks, but the details and effectiveness of this defense mechanism could be more thoroughly explored. Adding random noise may be an effective approach, but the magnitude and type of noise added, in addition to the choice of watermark, is often context-dependent.
*   **Novelty Incremental:** Although the paper builds on Tang et al. (2023), the extension to fine-tuning invariance is a novel contribution. This extends Tang's findings that apply to decoder networks in the context of autoencoders to the broader field of robust watermarking.

**Significance:**

The paper's significance lies in its attempt to provide a theoretically justified approach to robust neural network watermarking, particularly against fine-tuning. If validated by more extensive experiments, the method could offer a more reliable and principled way to protect the ownership of DNNs. The theoretical framework could also inspire further research into invariant features for watermarking and other security-related tasks.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper offers a sound theoretical base for robust watermarking by connecting it to the spectral properties of neural networks. The theoretical contribution is the most important finding in this paper and justifies some degree of impact. At the same time, the empirical results are limited and the reliance on low-frequency inputs is restrictive. Therefore, the novelty is not *exceptionally* high, and the *potential* significance is tempered by practical constraints. The contribution is also not exceptionally accessible to practitioners, due to the amount of required mathematical background.

**Score: 6**

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 6/10

### Where's the liability in the Generative Era? Recovery-based Black-Box Detection of AI-Generated Content
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.01008v1)
- **Authors**: Haoyue Bai, Yiyou Sun, Wei Cheng, Haifeng Chen
- **Abstract**: The recent proliferation of photorealistic images created by generative models has sparked both excitement and concern, as these images are increasingly indistinguishable from real ones to the human eye. While offering new creative and commercial possibilities, the potential for misuse, such as in misinformation and fraud, highlights the need for effective detection methods. Current detection approaches often rely on access to model weights or require extensive collections of real image datasets, limiting their scalability and practical application in real world scenarios. In this work, we introduce a novel black box detection framework that requires only API access, sidestepping the need for model weights or large auxiliary datasets. Our approach leverages a corrupt and recover strategy: by masking part of an image and assessing the model ability to reconstruct it, we measure the likelihood that the image was generated by the model itself. For black-box models that do not support masked image inputs, we incorporate a cost efficient surrogate model trained to align with the target model distribution, enhancing detection capability. Our framework demonstrates strong performance, outperforming baseline methods by 4.31% in mean average precision across eight diffusion model variant datasets.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and a critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary**

The paper introduces a novel "recovery-based black-box" framework for detecting AI-generated content (particularly images) without requiring access to the generative model's weights or large auxiliary datasets. The method works by corrupting a given image with a mask, then using either the target generative model (if accessible via API) or a trained "surrogate" model to reconstruct the masked area. The quality of reconstruction is then used as a signal to differentiate between real and fake images, under the assumption that generative models are better at recovering their own generated content. They demonstrate the method's effectiveness across a range of diffusion models and benchmark it against existing detection techniques.

**Critical Evaluation**

*   **Novelty:** The paper has decent novelty, especially in the black-box setting. The idea of using a "corrupt-and-recover" approach to detect generated images is interesting. Leveraging a surrogate model to align distributions when direct API access to masked inputs isn't available is a practical contribution.
    The theoretical insights on why this method works (likelihood-gap hypothesis) are also noteworthy and add value. The idea of using a "surrogate" generative model in conjunction with the corrupt-and-recover method for a black box scenario.
*   **Significance:** The significance stems from addressing the practical challenges of detecting AI-generated content in real-world scenarios where access to model internals is restricted.  Many existing methods rely on white-box access or substantial real-world datasets, limiting their applicability. This work offers a viable black-box alternative that is comparatively resource-efficient. The creation and release of a dataset specifically designed to challenge DALL-E 3 is a valuable contribution to the community.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Practicality:** The method is designed to be practical and usable with only API access, a crucial aspect for real-world deployment.
    *   **Resource efficiency:**  The paper emphasizes the method's efficiency, requiring relatively few API calls and minimal computational resources compared to prior works.
    *   **Generalizability:** The framework is shown to be effective across a diverse set of generative models, including the more recent and difficult-to-detect DALL-E 3.
    *   **Theoretical underpinning:** The "Likelihood-Gap Hypothesis" provides a theoretical explanation for why the method works, strengthening the paper's claims.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Dependence on surrogate model:** The performance in the black-box setting relies heavily on the surrogate model's ability to accurately mimic the target model's distribution.  The method's robustness to differences in model architectures or training data between the surrogate and target models could be further explored.
    *   **Scoring metric sensitivity:** The ablation study on scoring metrics reveals a high sensitivity to the chosen metric (PSNR).  This raises questions about the framework's stability and whether more robust, less sensitive metrics could be developed. The sensitivity to the choice of metric, while acknowledged, suggests that further refinement in metric selection is needed to increase the method's overall robustness.
    *   **Potential for adversarial attacks:** It's conceivable that an adversary could design generative models specifically to fool the reconstruction-based detection.
    *   **Limited human validation:** While the paper mentions a human preference evaluation, more extensive human studies, including diverse demographics, would add further weight to the method's reliability.

*   **Overall Impression:** The paper presents a useful and practical contribution to the field of AI-generated content detection. It addresses limitations of prior methods and offers a resource-efficient, black-box approach that shows promise.

**Score: 7.5**

**Rationale:** The paper has a good level of novelty by presenting a working black-box detection framework. The method is practical, and the inclusion of a DALL-E3 benchmark is quite useful for the community. However, there is a dependence on the surrogate model, and it is fairly sensitive to the choice of PSNR metric.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Improving Large Language Model Planning with Action Sequence Similarity
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.01009v1)
- **Authors**: Xinran Zhao, Hanie Sedghi, Bernd Bohnet, Dale Schuurmans, Azade Nova
- **Abstract**: Planning is essential for artificial intelligence systems to look ahead and proactively determine a course of actions to reach objectives in the virtual and real world. Recent work on large language models (LLMs) sheds light on their planning capability in various tasks. However, it remains unclear what signals in the context influence the model performance. In this work, we explore how to improve the model planning capability through in-context learning (ICL), specifically, what signals can help select the exemplars. Through extensive experiments, we observe that commonly used problem similarity may result in false positives with drastically different plans, which can mislead the model. In response, we propose to sample and filter exemplars leveraging plan side action sequence similarity (AS). We propose GRASE-DC: a two-stage pipeline that first re-samples high AS exemplars and then curates the selected exemplars with dynamic clustering on AS to achieve a balance of relevance and diversity. Our experimental result confirms that GRASE-DC achieves significant performance improvement on various planning tasks (up to ~11-40 point absolute accuracy improvement with 27.3% fewer exemplars needed on average). With GRASE-DC* + VAL, where we iteratively apply GRASE-DC with a validator, we are able to even boost the performance by 18.9% more. Extensive analysis validates the consistent performance improvement of GRASE-DC with various backbone LLMs and on both classical planning and natural language planning benchmarks. GRASE-DC can further boost the planning accuracy by ~24 absolute points on harder problems using simpler problems as exemplars over a random baseline. This demonstrates its ability to generalize to out-of-distribution problems.
- **Summary**: **Summary:**

This paper introduces GRASE-DC, a two-stage pipeline designed to improve the planning capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs) through in-context learning (ICL). The method addresses the problem of selecting appropriate demonstration examples (exemplars) by focusing on the action sequence similarity between plans. The first stage, Generative Re-sampling of Action Sequence Similar Exemplars (GRASE), re-samples exemplars based on their action sequence similarity to the model's initial plan for a given test task. The second stage, Dynamic Clustering (DC), refines this selection by clustering exemplars based on their pairwise action sequence similarity, promoting both relevance and diversity. The paper also presents GRASE-DC*, an iterative application of GRASE-DC. Experiments on both classical and natural language planning benchmarks demonstrate significant performance improvements with GRASE-DC, including higher accuracy and reduced exemplar count, compared to random exemplar selection. The paper explores variations with validation steps and demonstrates its effectiveness across different LLMs, achieving good results even in out-of-distribution scenarios. Furthermore, the authors propose methods to approximate the action sequence similarity to improve efficiency.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The core idea of using action sequence similarity (AS) rather than solely relying on task description similarity to select exemplars for LLM planning is moderately novel. Previous work in NLP often focuses on semantic similarity between input descriptions. While the idea of considering the "essence" of the plan through action sequences has intuitive appeal, the technical execution within the GRASE-DC pipeline is relatively straightforward. The dynamic clustering component adds a layer of refinement, but it builds on existing clustering techniques. The main novel contribution is the specific application of AS to exemplar selection in LLM planning and the empirical demonstration of its effectiveness. The iterative approach (GRASE-DC*) provides some further gains but is also a logical extension of the original approach.

**Significance:** The paper addresses a practically important problem: improving the planning abilities of LLMs. The demonstrated performance gains on multiple benchmarks are significant and suggest that the proposed approach can be a valuable tool for enhancing LLM-based agents. The reduction in the required number of exemplars directly translates to lower inference costs, a critical factor for deploying LLM-based systems. The robustness across different LLMs and the positive results in out-of-distribution settings increase the practical applicability of GRASE-DC. The analysis of efficiency trade-offs through approximations of AS using MLP and BPE is also valuable for real-world implementations.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the challenges of exemplar selection in LLM planning.
*   **Intuitive Approach:** The action sequence similarity approach is easy to understand and has a solid rationale.
*   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The experiments cover a wide range of tasks, LLMs, and settings, providing strong empirical evidence for the effectiveness of GRASE-DC.
*   **Efficiency Analysis:** The investigation of methods to approximate AS contributes to the practical usability of the approach.
*   **Iterative Refinement:** The GRASE-DC* extension and the experimental results highlighting consistent improvement adds further value.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Technical Depth:** The GRASE-DC pipeline is relatively simple, and the technical contributions are incremental. The method can be considered a clever application of existing techniques rather than a fundamental breakthrough.
*   **Limited Generalization Discussion:** While out-of-distribution tests are included, a deeper exploration of when and why GRASE-DC generalizes well would improve the theoretical contribution. More analysis of *which* actions are most important to match could further improve the process.
*   **Lack of Comparison with Advanced Retrieval Methods:**  While the paper compares with random selection and task similarity, it would be strengthened by comparison to more sophisticated semantic retrieval methods for exemplar selection (e.g., using embeddings of both task descriptions *and* potential plan sequences).
*   **Engineering Focus:** The paper has a strong engineering focus and lacks a more rigorous theoretical justification for the choice of action sequence similarity as the primary selection criterion.

**Overall:**

GRASE-DC offers a practical and effective approach to improve LLM planning through better exemplar selection. While the technical novelty might be somewhat limited, the empirical results and the potential impact on LLM-based agent performance justify the contribution. More advanced alternatives would be needed for a higher score.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### Do We Need a Detailed Rubric for Automated Essay Scoring using Large Language Models?
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.01035v1)
- **Authors**: Lui Yoshida
- **Abstract**: This study investigates the necessity and impact of a detailed rubric in automated essay scoring (AES) using large language models (LLMs). While using rubrics are standard in LLM-based AES, creating detailed rubrics requires substantial ef-fort and increases token usage. We examined how different levels of rubric detail affect scoring accuracy across multiple LLMs using the TOEFL11 dataset. Our experiments compared three conditions: a full rubric, a simplified rubric, and no rubric, using four different LLMs (Claude 3.5 Haiku, Gemini 1.5 Flash, GPT-4o-mini, and Llama 3 70B Instruct). Results showed that three out of four models maintained similar scoring accuracy with the simplified rubric compared to the detailed one, while significantly reducing token usage. However, one model (Gemini 1.5 Flash) showed decreased performance with more detailed rubrics. The findings suggest that simplified rubrics may be sufficient for most LLM-based AES applications, offering a more efficient alternative without compromis-ing scoring accuracy. However, model-specific evaluation remains crucial as per-formance patterns vary across different LLMs.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the impact of rubric detail on the performance of Large Language Models (LLMs) in automated essay scoring (AES).  It compares three rubric conditions (full, simplified, and no rubric) across four different LLMs (Claude 3.5 Haiku, Gemini 1.5 Flash, GPT-4o-mini, and Llama 3 70B Instruct) using the TOEFL11 dataset. The results suggest that for most LLMs tested, a simplified rubric performs comparably to a full rubric while reducing token usage and, therefore, cost and environmental impact.  One model (Gemini 1.5 Flash) exhibited decreased performance with more detailed rubrics. The study concludes that simplified rubrics may be sufficient for many LLM-based AES applications but emphasizes the need for model-specific evaluation due to varying performance patterns.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Practical Relevance:** The paper addresses a practical concern in LLM-based AES: the trade-off between rubric detail, performance, cost, and environmental considerations. The results directly inform prompt engineering strategies.
*   **Rigorous Methodology:** The study employs a well-defined methodology, including a commonly used dataset (TOEFL11), multiple LLMs, and statistical analysis (QWK with bootstrap resampling).
*   **Comparative Analysis:**  The paper directly compares different rubric conditions and highlights model-specific variations, contributing to a nuanced understanding of LLM behavior in AES.
*   **Clear Presentation:**  The paper is well-written, with clear explanations of the methods and results, and informative figures and tables.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Scope:** The study is limited to a specific dataset (TOEFL11) and a relatively small set of LLMs. The findings may not generalize to other datasets or models.  The prompts, while based on previous work, are not extensively optimized, leaving room for improvement.
*   **QWK limitations:** While QWK is a standard metric, its limitations are worth considering.  The conversion of 5-point rubric ratings to a 3-level scale might obscure finer-grained differences in scoring.
*   **Lack of Error Analysis:** While confusion matrices are included, a more in-depth error analysis (e.g., analyzing specific types of errors made by the LLMs under different conditions) would provide valuable insights.
*   **Novelty:** The concept of using LLMs for AES isn't novel; many other papers do that. Exploring the role of simplified vs. detailed rubrics contributes, but is a somewhat incremental improvement.

**Novelty and Significance:**

The paper's novelty lies in its systematic investigation of the impact of *rubric detail* on LLM-based AES performance. While using rubrics in LLM prompting is common, the question of how much detail is necessary and whether it's universally beneficial is less explored.  The finding that simplified rubrics can maintain accuracy while reducing token usage is practically significant for real-world applications. The model-specific performance variations also contribute valuable knowledge for practitioners choosing and configuring LLMs for AES.
The contribution is, however, incremental rather than revolutionary. It builds upon existing research and provides a more detailed understanding of a specific aspect of prompt engineering for AES. Other studies have already explored the use of LLMs for AES, and the benefits of using rubrics are generally well-known. The main contribution of this work is to specifically examine *how the detail in the rubric affects* performance across LLMs.

**Potential Influence:**

The paper has the potential to influence prompt engineering practices in LLM-based AES.  The findings could encourage practitioners to experiment with simplified rubrics and conduct model-specific evaluations to optimize for both accuracy and efficiency.  It also highlights the importance of not blindly relying on detailed rubrics, particularly with certain models.

**Score:** 6

**Justification:**

The paper presents a solid and relevant investigation with practical implications for the field of automated essay scoring. However, the limited scope of the experiments (particularly the dataset and models used) and the incremental nature of the contribution prevent it from achieving a higher score. More extensive experiments, additional error analysis, or a novel approach to rubric design would have strengthened the paper significantly. Nonetheless, the results are valuable for researchers and practitioners working on LLM-based AES and warrant a solid contribution score.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### Low-Precision Training of Large Language Models: Methods, Challenges, and Opportunities
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.01043v1)
- **Authors**: Zhiwei Hao, Jianyuan Guo, Li Shen, Yong Luo, Han Hu, Guoxia Wang, Dianhai Yu, Yonggang Wen, Dacheng Tao
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) have achieved impressive performance across various domains. However, the substantial hardware resources required for their training present a significant barrier to efficiency and scalability. To mitigate this challenge, low-precision training techniques have been widely adopted, leading to notable advancements in training efficiency. Despite these gains, low-precision training involves several components$\unicode{x2013}$such as weights, activations, and gradients$\unicode{x2013}$each of which can be represented in different numerical formats. The resulting diversity has created a fragmented landscape in low-precision training research, making it difficult for researchers to gain a unified overview of the field. This survey provides a comprehensive review of existing low-precision training methods. To systematically organize these approaches, we categorize them into three primary groups based on their underlying numerical formats, which is a key factor influencing hardware compatibility, computational efficiency, and ease of reference for readers. The categories are: (1) fixed-point and integer-based methods, (2) floating-point-based methods, and (3) customized format-based methods. Additionally, we discuss quantization-aware training approaches, which share key similarities with low-precision training during forward propagation. Finally, we highlight several promising research directions to advance this field. A collection of papers discussed in this survey is provided in https://github.com/Hao840/Awesome-Low-Precision-Training.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper abstract:

**Summary:**

The paper is a survey on low-precision training techniques for large language models (LLMs). It addresses the increasing computational cost and scalability challenges associated with training large models by reviewing methods that reduce the numerical precision of weights, activations, and gradients. The survey categorizes these methods into fixed-point and integer-based, floating-point-based, and customized format-based approaches. The authors also discuss quantization-aware training (QAT) and system-level support and highlight promising research directions in the field. A companion website "Awesome-Low-Precision-Training" provides a list of the papers discussed in the survey.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies primarily in its comprehensive organization and synthesis of existing low-precision training methods. While individual techniques are not novel, the categorization of approaches by numerical format provides a structured overview that has been lacking in the fragmented landscape of low-precision training research. The inclusion of QAT and system support perspectives adds to the comprehensiveness. A website containing all papers discussed is also a practical contribution.

*   **Significance:** Low-precision training is crucial for democratizing LLM development, making it accessible to researchers and practitioners with limited resources. A well-organized survey can accelerate research and development efforts by providing a clear understanding of the trade-offs and capabilities of different techniques. The paper clearly articulates the advantages of low-precision training (reduced memory, faster computation, lower communication overhead) and its increasing importance with growing model sizes.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Comprehensive Coverage:** The survey appears to cover a wide range of low-precision training techniques.
    *   **Clear Categorization:** Organizing methods by numerical format offers a logical structure that aids understanding and comparison.
    *   **Practical Resource:** The "Awesome-Low-Precision-Training" companion website serves as a valuable resource for researchers.
    *   **Focus on LLMs:** The emphasis on LLMs is timely and relevant given the current state of AI research.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Lack of In-depth Analysis (Based on Abstract):** The abstract indicates a review, but it's unclear how deeply the survey analyzes the strengths and weaknesses of each approach beyond basic descriptions. A truly impactful survey would offer critical comparisons and insights that go beyond summarization.
    *   **Limited Discussion of Hardware:** The abstract mentions that numerical format is a "key factor influencing hardware compatibility". The survey would be stronger if it contained a deeper discussion of how specific low-precision formats map to different hardware architectures and their respective performance characteristics.
    *   **Potential for Rapid Obsolescence:** The field of low-precision training is rapidly evolving. The survey's value will diminish over time if it's not regularly updated.

*   **Potential Influence:** A well-executed survey in this domain can significantly influence the direction of future research by identifying gaps, highlighting promising approaches, and promoting a more unified understanding of the field.

*   **Justification of Score:** The paper provides a valuable service to the community by organizing a complex and rapidly evolving field. However, based on the abstract, the novelty is incremental (organization of existing work), and the analysis may lack depth. A higher score would be justified if the survey provided novel insights or a particularly insightful comparative analysis.

Score: 7

**Rigorous Rationale:**

A score of 7 reflects the paper's importance as a comprehensive resource and its value in organizing the field of low-precision training. However, the incremental novelty and potential lack of in-depth analysis, as suggested by the abstract, prevent a higher score. The paper has the potential to influence research directions by providing a more unified understanding of the field, but its long-term impact will depend on the depth of its analysis and its ability to stay current with future advances. This score also indicates a solid contribution and a useful synthesis, but that the work falls short of true breakthrough innovation or particularly insightful analysis.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Multi-Step Consistency Models: Fast Generation with Theoretical Guarantees
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.01049v1)
- **Authors**: Nishant Jain, Xunpeng Huang, Yian Ma, Tong Zhang
- **Abstract**: Consistency models have recently emerged as a compelling alternative to traditional SDE based diffusion models, offering a significant acceleration in generation by producing high quality samples in very few steps. Despite their empirical success, a proper theoretic justification for their speed up is still lacking. In this work, we provide the analysis which bridges this gap, showing that given a consistency model which can map the input at a given time to arbitrary timestamps along the reverse trajectory, one can achieve KL divergence of order $ O(\varepsilon^2) $ using only $ O\left(\log\left(\frac{d}{\varepsilon}\right)\right) $ iterations with constant step size, where d is the data dimension. Additionally, under minimal assumptions on the data distribution an increasingly common setting in recent diffusion model analyses we show that a similar KL convergence guarantee can be obtained, with the number of steps scaling as $ O\left(d \log\left(\frac{d}{\varepsilon}\right)\right) $. Going further, we also provide a theoretical analysis for estimation of such consistency models, concluding that accurate learning is feasible using small discretization steps, both in smooth and non smooth settings. Notably, our results for the non smooth case yield best in class convergence rates compared to existing SDE or ODE based analyses under minimal assumptions.
- **Summary**: This paper provides theoretical analysis for multi-step generation using consistency models. It shows that, under certain assumptions, the number of iterations required for probability flow ODE based generation can be independent of epsilon (the desired accuracy) to generate samples from a distribution that is epsilon^2-close in KL divergence to the target distribution. The paper achieves O(d log(1/epsilon)) convergence, which they claim is state-of-the-art with respect to both dimension *d* and epsilon *e*, compared to O(1/epsilon) convergence in current ODE/SDE based samplers. The authors provide analysis under two sets of assumptions: one with smoothness assumptions on the score function and another without. They also provide theoretical analysis for estimating the consistency function.

**Critical Evaluation of Novelty and Significance:**

The paper makes a contribution by providing theoretical guarantees for consistency models, which are known to provide fast generation. The key novelty lies in achieving a convergence rate that is independent of epsilon, enabling faster sampling compared to prior SDE/ODE based methods, especially in high-dimensional settings. By doing away with epsilon as a dependency, the authors are able to show that the consistency property of these models can be exploited for very fast inference. The authors have relaxed constraints and have come up with an expression which is an improvement over DDPM in terms of number of steps required.

However, the paper also has some limitations. The bound on hy (step size) affects the analysis.  The assumptions about the score function estimation error and smoothness are standard but might be restrictive in certain practical scenarios.  The practical implications of these theoretical improvements need further empirical validation to demonstrate the actual performance gains, as the constant factors in the log(1/epsilon) term could be significant. The analysis also requires an efficient ODE solver. Also, as discussed, the lower bound is dependent on constant factors which needs to be carefully tuned.

The work is significant because it addresses an important aspect of diffusion models: speeding up the sampling process. While empirical results for consistency models are promising, theoretical justification is crucial for understanding their behavior and limitations. The paper’s findings can potentially guide the design of more efficient sampling strategies and training objectives for consistency models. It also paves the way for further theoretical research exploring the properties of consistency models and their relationship to other generative models.

Score: 7

Justification:

The paper presents a significant advance in the theoretical understanding of consistency models, providing tight convergence rates and relaxing some assumptions present in previous analyses. The achieved O(d log(1/epsilon)) convergence rate is a notable improvement with strong theoretical backing. The detailed learning analysis is a bonus, even though it reuses prior results.

However, the paper isn't groundbreaking enough for a higher score due to the reasons below:

1.  The results are highly dependent on the assumptions made, especially concerning the smoothness or lack thereof of the score function. If these assumptions are invalid or don't translate into practical improvements, the theoretical guarantees become less compelling.
2.  The empirical validation to connect the theoretical improvements to actual sampling performance needs to be fleshed out with more detailed arguments and analysis. While they provide detailed theoretical bounds, they remain theoretical and can be further evaluated based on various aspects, thereby strengthening the practical implications of the result.
3.  The practical tunability of the theoretical constants/parameters in the given algorithm (multi-step consistency generation) needs further exploration.

Overall, the paper offers a solid contribution to the theoretical literature and provides a good basis for future research. The importance and direct usability of the theoretical bounds remains to be seen, and thus, I assign a slightly conservative score.
Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Transferable Adversarial Attacks on Black-Box Vision-Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.01050v1)
- **Authors**: Kai Hu, Weichen Yu, Li Zhang, Alexander Robey, Andy Zou, Chengming Xu, Haoqi Hu, Matt Fredrikson
- **Abstract**: Vision Large Language Models (VLLMs) are increasingly deployed to offer advanced capabilities on inputs comprising both text and images. While prior research has shown that adversarial attacks can transfer from open-source to proprietary black-box models in text-only and vision-only contexts, the extent and effectiveness of such vulnerabilities remain underexplored for VLLMs. We present a comprehensive analysis demonstrating that targeted adversarial examples are highly transferable to widely-used proprietary VLLMs such as GPT-4o, Claude, and Gemini. We show that attackers can craft perturbations to induce specific attacker-chosen interpretations of visual information, such as misinterpreting hazardous content as safe, overlooking sensitive or restricted material, or generating detailed incorrect responses aligned with the attacker's intent. Furthermore, we discover that universal perturbations -- modifications applicable to a wide set of images -- can consistently induce these misinterpretations across multiple proprietary VLLMs. Our experimental results on object recognition, visual question answering, and image captioning show that this vulnerability is common across current state-of-the-art models, and underscore an urgent need for robust mitigations to ensure the safe and secure deployment of VLLMs.
- **Summary**: The paper "Transferable Adversarial Attacks on Black-Box Vision-Language Models" investigates the vulnerability of Vision Large Language Models (VLLMs) to transferable adversarial attacks. The authors demonstrate that adversarial examples crafted on open-source models can effectively transfer to proprietary black-box VLLMs like GPT-4o, Claude, and Gemini, causing them to misinterpret visual information, overlook sensitive content, or generate incorrect responses. The paper proposes methods to improve the transferability of these attacks through techniques at the model, loss function, and data levels, including model ensembling, visual contrastive loss, and data augmentation. They evaluate their approach on tasks like object recognition, image captioning, and visual question answering, showing significant attack success rates against state-of-the-art models.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The paper tackles an important and timely problem: the security risks associated with VLLMs, especially in black-box scenarios.
    *   The authors conduct a comprehensive analysis demonstrating the transferability of adversarial attacks across different VLLMs and tasks.
    *   The proposed techniques for improving attack transferability are well-motivated and experimentally validated.
    *   The empirical results are convincing, showing significant attack success rates against widely-used proprietary models.
    *   The ablation studies provide insights into the factors that contribute to multi-modal transferability.
    *   The identification of the discrepancy in alignment robustness as the main cause of the vulnerability is a good find.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   While the paper explores several techniques for improving transferability, some of the individual improvements are somewhat incremental. The gain comes through the whole pipeline.
    *   The current results remain visually noticeable due to the large perturbation norm bound.
    *   The threat model is a standard one, with white box access to surrogate models.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**
    *   While adversarial attacks on image and text models have been extensively studied, the analysis of transferable attacks in the context of VLLMs, particularly against black-box proprietary models, is a relatively unexplored area.
    *   The paper provides a comprehensive experimental evaluation on different VLLMs and tasks, which sheds light on the vulnerabilities of these models.
    *   The proposed techniques for improving transferability, such as model ensembling, visual contrastive loss, and data augmentation, could be valuable for future research on adversarial attacks and defenses for VLLMs.
    *   The demonstration of universal perturbations is an important result, highlighting the potential for widespread vulnerability.

*   **Potential Influence:**
    *   The findings of this paper could raise awareness among developers and users of VLLMs about the potential security risks associated with these models.
    *   The proposed techniques for improving transferability could be used to develop more effective adversarial attacks and defenses for VLLMs.
    *   The paper could motivate further research on understanding and mitigating the vulnerabilities of VLLMs in real-world applications.

**Justification:**

This paper delivers a comprehensive analysis of adversarial attacks in the context of black-box VLLMs. It presents a targeted solution, offering clear improvements in transferability. It contributes meaningfully to the evolving field, addressing a timely problem and identifying specific techniques to enhance adversarial attacks on proprietary VLLMs. Despite not being a completely groundbreaking theoretical contribution, the thorough experimental results, valuable insights into contributing factors to multimodal transferability, and clear demonstration of the vulnerability across different models justify a high score.

**Score: 7.5**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Efficient Vocabulary-Free Fine-Grained Visual Recognition in the Age of Multimodal LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.01064v1)
- **Authors**: Hari Chandana Kuchibhotla, Sai Srinivas Kancheti, Abbavaram Gowtham Reddy, Vineeth N Balasubramanian
- **Abstract**: Fine-grained Visual Recognition (FGVR) involves distinguishing between visually similar categories, which is inherently challenging due to subtle inter-class differences and the need for large, expert-annotated datasets. In domains like medical imaging, such curated datasets are unavailable due to issues like privacy concerns and high annotation costs. In such scenarios lacking labeled data, an FGVR model cannot rely on a predefined set of training labels, and hence has an unconstrained output space for predictions. We refer to this task as Vocabulary-Free FGVR (VF-FGVR), where a model must predict labels from an unconstrained output space without prior label information. While recent Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) show potential for VF-FGVR, querying these models for each test input is impractical because of high costs and prohibitive inference times. To address these limitations, we introduce \textbf{Nea}rest-Neighbor Label \textbf{R}efinement (NeaR), a novel approach that fine-tunes a downstream CLIP model using labels generated by an MLLM. Our approach constructs a weakly supervised dataset from a small, unlabeled training set, leveraging MLLMs for label generation. NeaR is designed to handle the noise, stochasticity, and open-endedness inherent in labels generated by MLLMs, and establishes a new benchmark for efficient VF-FGVR.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the problem of fine-grained visual recognition (FGVR) in situations where labeled training data is scarce or unavailable. They coin this problem as "Vocabulary-Free FGVR" (VF-FGVR). The authors note that directly using Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) for inference on each test image is computationally expensive. To alleviate this, they propose Nearest-Neighbor Label Refinement (NeaR), a method that fine-tunes a downstream CLIP model using labels generated by an MLLM on a small unlabeled training set.  NeaR constructs candidate label sets for each image based on the labels of its nearest neighbors, partitions the data into clean and noisy samples, and refines labels accordingly.  Label filtering is also incorporated to truncate the label space and improve efficiency. The paper demonstrates that NeaR achieves performance comparable to or exceeding direct MLLM inference at a significantly reduced computational cost.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The paper's novelty lies primarily in the specific combination of existing techniques to tackle the VF-FGVR problem.  Using MLLMs for label generation is not entirely new, nor is the concept of learning with noisy labels.  However, the proposed NeaR approach effectively combines:
    *   MLLM-based label generation for weakly supervised learning.
    *   Nearest-neighbor based candidate set construction to mitigate label noise.
    *   Partitioning data into clean and noisy samples via a GMM for selective label refinement.
    *   Label filtering to address the open-ended nature of MLLM outputs and improve efficiency.
    The combination of these elements, particularly in the context of VF-FGVR, represents a distinct contribution.

*   **Significance:**  The paper addresses a practically relevant problem: the scarcity of labeled data in fine-grained recognition scenarios, especially in specialized domains.  The proposed method offers a cost-effective alternative to directly querying large MLLMs, making FGVR more accessible in resource-constrained environments. The performance gains over existing VF-FGVR methods, and even some direct MLLM baselines, highlight the effectiveness of NeaR. The results in Table 1 demonstrate a significant reduction in cost and inference time compared to direct GPT-40 inference.  The comprehensive experimental evaluation across several fine-grained datasets provides strong empirical support for the proposed method.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The paper provides a clear problem definition and motivation.
    *   The proposed NeaR method is well-explained and justified.
    *   The experimental evaluation is thorough, with comparisons against relevant baselines, ablation studies, and variations in experimental settings.
    *   The paper addresses practical concerns such as computational cost and efficiency.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The individual components of NeaR are not entirely novel. The novelty comes from the specific arrangement. While this is clear, it is essential to highlight this clearly.
    *   The reliance on a CLIP model as a downstream classifier could be seen as a limitation, as it ties the method to a specific architecture. The supplementary material demonstrates the method still works with different CLIP backbones.
    *   The choice of hyperparameters (e.g., к, GMM components, temperature) could influence performance, and while the paper explores variations, more detailed analysis could be warranted.

*   **Potential Influence:**  The paper could influence future research in weakly supervised FGVR, particularly in leveraging MLLMs for label generation and handling noisy or open-ended label spaces. The NeaR approach could be extended to other visual recognition tasks or adapted to different downstream classifiers. The benchmark established for VF-FGVR, particularly with practical considerations of computational cost, may encourage further development of efficient and scalable solutions.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper presents a novel and effective method for addressing a practically important problem. While the individual components of NeaR are not entirely new, their specific combination and application to VF-FGVR demonstrate a clear contribution. The comprehensive experimental evaluation and the focus on computational efficiency strengthen the paper's significance. While the reliance on CLIP could be seen as a limitation, the benefits derived from the approach significantly outweigh this. The potential influence on future research in weakly supervised FGVR is considerable, making the paper a valuable contribution to the field.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Good News for Script Kiddies? Evaluating Large Language Models for Automated Exploit Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.01065v1)
- **Authors**: David Jin, Qian Fu, Yuekang Li
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) have demonstrated remarkable capabilities in code-related tasks, raising concerns about their potential for automated exploit generation (AEG). This paper presents the first systematic study on LLMs' effectiveness in AEG, evaluating both their cooperativeness and technical proficiency. To mitigate dataset bias, we introduce a benchmark with refactored versions of five software security labs. Additionally, we design an LLM-based attacker to systematically prompt LLMs for exploit generation. Our experiments reveal that GPT-4 and GPT-4o exhibit high cooperativeness, comparable to uncensored models, while Llama3 is the most resistant. However, no model successfully generates exploits for refactored labs, though GPT-4o's minimal errors highlight the potential for LLM-driven AEG advancements.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper, "Good News for Script Kiddies? Evaluating Large Language Models for Automated Exploit Generation."

**Summary:**

The paper presents an empirical study evaluating the capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs) in Automated Exploit Generation (AEG).  The authors address concerns about the accessibility of exploitation techniques due to LLMs.  To mitigate dataset bias, they created a benchmark by refactoring existing security lab exercises (SEED Labs).  They developed an LLM-based attacker to systematically prompt and guide target LLMs in generating exploits.  The study assesses the LLMs' cooperativeness and technical proficiency. The results indicate that while certain models like GPT-4 and GPT-4o exhibit a high degree of cooperation, none successfully generated exploits for the refactored labs. GPT-40's minimal errors, however, suggest potential for future LLM-driven AEG advancements. The authors provide a new benchmark and the LLM-based attacker to support future research.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Novelty:** This paper addresses an interesting and timely research question that hasn't been addressed systematically before. Evaluating the potential of LLMs for AEG is a crucial area of investigation given the growing power and availability of these models. The first systematic approach to this problem is in itself novel.
    *   **Methodology:** The paper makes an effort to address dataset bias by refactoring the SEED Labs. This is a crucial step, as training on the labs would artificially inflate LLMs performance. The LLM-based attacker is a strong contribution as it automates the interaction process, reducing bias and improving reproducibility.
    *   **Clarity and Reproducibility:** The paper is well-written and clearly explains the experimental setup and results. The authors contribute an important benchmark and code for future research, enhancing reproducibility and facilitating follow-up studies.
    *   **Relevance:** The topic is highly relevant to both the software security and AI research communities.  Understanding the potential for LLMs to lower the barrier to entry for exploit development is essential for proactively addressing security threats.
    *   **Detailed Analysis:** The paper provides a detailed analysis of the types of errors that the LLMs made. This is valuable for understanding the limitations of current LLMs and for guiding future research on how to improve their AEG capabilities.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Scope of Benchmarks:** While refactoring SEED Labs is a good starting point, the number of labs considered and the variety of vulnerabilities might be limited. SEED Labs are somewhat basic and might not fully represent the complexity of real-world vulnerabilities. Further studies using more varied and complex vulnerabilities are needed to fully assess LLMs' AEG capabilities.
    *   **Attacker LLM Configuration:** The configuration and performance of the LLM-based attacker is a key component, but the reasoning behind selecting GPT-4o, and its performance characteristics are not adequately justified or analyzed in the paper. The attacker LLM introduces a potential for confounding variables, making it difficult to definitively attribute the target LLM's performance solely to its AEG skills. A more rigorous analysis of the attacker LLM's influence is necessary.
    *   **Lack of Real-World Validation:** The study focuses on synthetic labs. While this allows for controlled experiments, it does not necessarily translate to real-world scenarios where vulnerabilities are often less well-defined and require more complex reasoning and analysis. The paper would benefit from including experiments on real-world vulnerabilities to assess the generalizability of the findings.
    *   **Limited Model Evaluation:** The models assessed only represent a fraction of LLMs. The choice of models is reasonably justified, but further experiments including fine-tuned or more specialized models could strengthen the conclusions. It's important to understand how different architectures and training strategies affect AEG performance.

*   **Significance:**

    *   The paper's significance lies in its pioneering nature.  It's the first systematic attempt to evaluate LLMs for AEG, providing a baseline for future research.  The results highlight the limitations of current LLMs in this domain but also suggest potential for future advancements. The creation of a curated benchmark and an LLM-based attacker are valuable resources that can be used by other researchers.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper makes a valuable contribution by addressing a timely and important research question. Its novel methodology, including the refactored benchmarks and the LLM-based attacker, represents a significant step forward in evaluating LLMs for AEG. While the study has limitations, particularly in the scope of vulnerabilities and real-world validation, its systematic approach, detailed analysis, and the released benchmark make it a significant contribution. It identifies current limitations, presents a methodology to systematically evaluate LLMs in AEG and establishes an initial benchmark. Therefore, this paper scores between a 6 and a 7.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CR
- **Score**: 7/10

### A Rusty Link in the AI Supply Chain: Detecting Evil Configurations in Model Repositories
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.01067v1)
- **Authors**: Ziqi Ding, Qian Fu, Junchen Ding, Gelei Deng, Yi Liu, Yuekang Li
- **Abstract**: Recent advancements in large language models (LLMs) have spurred the development of diverse AI applications from code generation and video editing to text generation; however, AI supply chains such as Hugging Face, which host pretrained models and their associated configuration files contributed by the public, face significant security challenges; in particular, configuration files originally intended to set up models by specifying parameters and initial settings can be exploited to execute unauthorized code, yet research has largely overlooked their security compared to that of the models themselves; in this work, we present the first comprehensive study of malicious configurations on Hugging Face, identifying three attack scenarios (file, website, and repository operations) that expose inherent risks; to address these threats, we introduce CONFIGSCAN, an LLM-based tool that analyzes configuration files in the context of their associated runtime code and critical libraries, effectively detecting suspicious elements with low false positive rates and high accuracy; our extensive evaluation uncovers thousands of suspicious repositories and configuration files, underscoring the urgent need for enhanced security validation in AI model hosting platforms.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "A Rusty Link in the AI Supply Chain: Detecting Evil Configurations in Model Repositories" addresses the overlooked security risks associated with configuration files in AI model hosting platforms like Hugging Face.  It identifies three attack scenarios (file, website, and repository operations) where malicious actors can exploit these configuration files to execute unauthorized code. To mitigate these risks, the authors introduce CONFIGSCAN, an LLM-based tool designed to analyze configuration files within the context of their associated runtime code and critical libraries, detecting suspicious elements with high accuracy and low false positive rates.  The paper presents an evaluation of CONFIGSCAN, demonstrating its ability to identify suspicious repositories and configuration files, thus highlighting the urgent need for enhanced security validation in AI model hosting platforms.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The primary strength of this paper lies in its focus on configuration files within AI model repositories, an area largely unexplored in prior research.  Existing works primarily target model poisoning or adversarial attacks *on the models themselves*.  Shifting the attention to configuration files as a potential attack vector is a novel and valuable contribution. The idea of using an LLM based approach to analyze configuration files considering the context is also novel.

*   **Significance:** The identified attack scenarios are realistic and represent a genuine threat to AI supply chains.  The potential for attackers to exploit configuration files for file operations, website operations, and repository redirection could lead to serious security breaches, including data theft, remote code execution, and distribution of poisoned models. The paper effectively demonstrates that current security measures are insufficient to address these threats. It highlights how rule based systems are limited in their capacity to detect complex vulnerabilities.

*   **Methodology:** The methodology involves a combination of rule-based analysis and LLM-based analysis. The rule-based analysis serves as a pre-filter, and the LLM-based analysis provides deeper semantic understanding. The CONFIGSCAN tool is well-described and the approach is clearly laid out.
*   **Evaluation:** While the authors conducted experiments, the evaluation section presents a potential weakness. The initial rule-based analysis is applied to 150,000 repositories, uncovering a substantial number of suspicious repositories and configuration files. However, the evaluation of CONFIGSCAN is only performed on a *sample* of 1,000 repositories. The absence of a more rigorous comparison of CONGIFSCAN and rule-based approach on the complete dataset weakens the claims of improvement. The paper also identifies only two new repositories containing vulnerabilities. While promising, it is not enough to evaluate CONFIGSCAN's real-world impact.
*   **Presentation and Clarity:** The paper is well-written and clearly structured. The problem is clearly defined, the approach is well explained, and the results are presented in a logical manner.

*   **Limitations:**
    *   The reliance on LLMs introduces potential limitations, including the possibility of hallucinations or biases. While the authors mention this and try to address it, a more thorough discussion of the LLM's prompt engineering, evaluation of its accuracy, and potential biases is warranted.
    *   The scope of the evaluation is limited. A larger-scale evaluation, particularly focusing on a more diverse set of repositories and attack scenarios, would significantly strengthen the findings.
    *   The absence of comparisons of CONFIGSCAN's runtime or resource utilization compared to other methods.

**Justification for Score:**

While the paper presents a novel and significant problem, the evaluation is not sufficiently robust to fully demonstrate the effectiveness of CONFIGSCAN. The vulnerability detection improvements compared to the rule-based approach are not completely conclusive. The novelty of focusing on configuration files in AI supply chains justifies a reasonable score.

Score: 6.5

- **Classification**: cs.CR
- **Score**: 6/10

### Multimodal Transformers are Hierarchical Modal-wise Heterogeneous Graphs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.01068v1)
- **Authors**: Yijie Jin, Junjie Peng, Xuanchao Lin, Haochen Yuan, Lan Wang, Cangzhi Zheng
- **Abstract**: Multimodal Sentiment Analysis (MSA) is a rapidly developing field that integrates multimodal information to recognize sentiments, and existing models have made significant progress in this area. The central challenge in MSA is multimodal fusion, which is predominantly addressed by Multimodal Transformers (MulTs). Although act as the paradigm, MulTs suffer from efficiency concerns. In this work, from the perspective of efficiency optimization, we propose and prove that MulTs are hierarchical modal-wise heterogeneous graphs (HMHGs), and we introduce the graph-structured representation pattern of MulTs. Based on this pattern, we propose an Interlaced Mask (IM) mechanism to design the Graph-Structured and Interlaced-Masked Multimodal Transformer (GsiT). It is formally equivalent to MulTs which achieves an efficient weight-sharing mechanism without information disorder through IM, enabling All-Modal-In-One fusion with only 1/3 of the parameters of pure MulTs. A Triton kernel called Decomposition is implemented to ensure avoiding additional computational overhead. Moreover, it achieves significantly higher performance than traditional MulTs. To further validate the effectiveness of GsiT itself and the HMHG concept, we integrate them into multiple state-of-the-art models and demonstrate notable performance improvements and parameter reduction on widely used MSA datasets.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces GsiT (Graph-Structured Interlaced-Masked Multimodal Transformer), a more efficient variant of Multimodal Transformers (MulTs) for Multimodal Sentiment Analysis (MSA). The authors prove that MulTs are hierarchical modal-wise heterogeneous graphs (HMHGs) and leverage this understanding to design GsiT with an Interlaced Mask (IM) mechanism.  This allows for All-Modal-In-One fusion with fewer parameters (1/3 of MulTs) and a theoretically equivalent performance.  A custom Triton kernel ("Decomposition") is also developed to maintain efficiency and avoid computational overhead.  The paper demonstrates GsiT's superiority over traditional MulTs and state-of-the-art methods on widely used MSA datasets. The HMHG concept is also integrated into existing models to further validate its effectiveness.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in several key aspects:

    *   **HMHG Representation:** The formalization of MulTs as hierarchical modal-wise heterogeneous graphs (HMHGs) is a significant theoretical contribution. This perspective provides a deeper understanding of the model's structure and function.
    *   **Interlaced Mask (IM) Mechanism:** The IM mechanism for multimodal weight sharing, enabling All-Modal-In-One fusion, is a novel architectural innovation. This is the core of the efficiency gains.
    *   **Decomposition Kernel:** The development of a Triton kernel to efficiently implement the weight sharing and masking is crucial for practical implementation and addresses potential computational bottlenecks.
*   **Significance:**

    *   **Efficiency Improvement:** Addressing the efficiency concerns of MulTs is highly significant for the practical application of MSA. The reduction in parameters and the maintenance of computational efficiency are valuable contributions.
    *   **Performance Enhancement:** The performance improvements on benchmark datasets demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed approach and its potential to advance the state-of-the-art in MSA.
    *   **Theoretical Foundation:** The solid theoretical grounding (HMHG representation, proof of equivalence) strengthens the credibility of the work.
*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Strong Theoretical Justification:** The paper offers a rigorous mathematical analysis and proof of the HMHG theorem. This provides a solid foundation for the GsiT architecture.
    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly identifies the efficiency concerns of MulTs and proposes a well-defined solution.
    *   **Comprehensive Experimental Evaluation:** The experiments are thorough and include comparisons with state-of-the-art methods on multiple datasets. The evaluation also considers both performance and efficiency metrics.
    *   **Code Availability:** The source code availability enhances the reproducibility and adoption of the proposed method.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Complexity of Implementation:** The Triton kernel (Decomposition) suggests that implementing GsiT might be technically challenging, potentially limiting its immediate widespread adoption.
    *   **Limited Modalities:** The paper mainly focuses on text, vision, and audio. The effectiveness of GsiT on other modalities, such as sensor data or physiological signals, is not explored.
    *   **Lack of analysis on dataset imbalance:** The datasets used in the experiments had dataset imbalance. Although the metric 'W' was used to address the issue, a more detailed discussion on how GsiT can be adopted when facing such an issue in different scenarios could be added.
*   **Potential Influence:**

    *   The paper has the potential to influence future research in MSA by providing a more efficient and theoretically grounded alternative to traditional MulTs.
    *   The HMHG concept could be applied to other multimodal tasks beyond sentiment analysis.
    *   The IM mechanism could inspire new architectures for weight sharing in deep learning models.

**Overall Assessment:**

The paper presents a significant contribution to the field of Multimodal Sentiment Analysis. The theoretical analysis, architectural innovation, and thorough experimental evaluation make it a valuable addition to the literature. While the implementation complexity and limited modalities are potential drawbacks, the efficiency gains and performance improvements justify a strong score.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### Retrieval Augmented Learning: A Retrial-based Large Language Model Self-Supervised Learning and Autonomous Knowledge Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.01073v1)
- **Authors**: Zongyuan Li, Pengfei Li, Runnan Qi, Yanan Ni, Lumin Jiang, Hui Wu, Xuebo Zhang, Kuihua Huang, Xian Guo
- **Abstract**: The lack of domain-specific data in the pre-training of Large Language Models (LLMs) severely limits LLM-based decision systems in specialized applications, while post-training a model in the scenarios requires significant computational resources. In this paper, we present Retrial-Augmented Learning (RAL), a reward-free self-supervised learning framework for LLMs that operates without model training. By developing Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) into a module for organizing intermediate data, we realized a three-stage autonomous knowledge generation of proposing a hypothesis, validating the hypothesis, and generating the knowledge. The method is evaluated in the LLM-PySC2 environment, a representative decision-making platform that combines sufficient complexity with domain-specific knowledge requirements. Experiments demonstrate that the proposed method effectively reduces hallucination by generating and utilizing validated knowledge, and increases decision-making performance at an extremely low cost. Meanwhile, the approach exhibits potential in out-of-distribution(OOD) tasks, robustness, and transferability, making it a cost-friendly but effective solution for decision-making problems and autonomous knowledge generation.
- **Summary**: Okay, I've analyzed the provided paper. Here's a summary and a critical evaluation:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Retrial-Augmented Learning (RAL), a novel reward-free self-supervised learning framework designed to enhance the decision-making capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs) in specialized applications. RAL operates without model training and leverages Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) to organize intermediate data in a three-stage process: hypothesis proposal, hypothesis validation, and knowledge generation.  The approach is evaluated in the LLM-PySC2 environment, demonstrating reduced hallucination, increased decision-making performance, and potential for out-of-distribution tasks, robustness, and transferability, all at a low computational cost. The paper claims that RAL presents a cost-effective solution for decision-making problems and autonomous knowledge generation.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of RAL, combining RAG with a retrial-based self-supervised learning loop for LLMs *without* requiring fine-tuning, appears to have some novelty. Existing works often rely on prompt engineering, multi-agent workflows, or knowledge from fixed datasets, and while LLM reflection is related, it still faces hallucination issues. The novelty lies in the specific implementation of the hypothesis-validation-knowledge generation cycle *using* RAG and the claim that this significantly reduces hallucination. The paper argues a departure from prior approaches for autonomous knowledge generation with low computing resources. However, components like RAG and LLM reflection are established; it's the combination and application that bring potential novelty.

*   **Significance:** If the claims hold up (and that's a big "if" we will get to in the weakness section), the significance could be considerable. LLMs, particularly when used for decision-making, suffer from domain knowledge gaps and are often computationally expensive to adapt. A train-free, reward-free learning method *that is also effective* would be highly desirable, especially for resource-constrained environments and edge devices. Reducing hallucinations is a persistent challenge, and RAL offers a potentially promising avenue. The significance comes from the *potential* to make LLM-based decision systems more accessible and reliable.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Problem Statement:** The paper clearly identifies the limitations of existing LLM decision-making systems, specifically concerning domain knowledge and computational cost.

    *   **Well-Defined Framework:** RAL is presented with a clear structure and its components (hypothesis proposal, validation, knowledge generation) are well-described.
    *   **Empirical Evaluation:** The use of the LLM-PySC2 environment provides a standardized platform for evaluation. Experiments are conducted for OOD tasks, robustness, and transferability.
    *   **Discussion of Limitations:** The paper acknowledges the limitations of its approach, particularly concerning insufficient exploration and validation for long-horizon policies.
    *   **Comparative Evaluation:** The paper includes baselines and ablations of reflection methods to analyze the impact of the research.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Technical Depth in the RAL Mechanism:** Although described clearly, the actual implementation of the three stages (proposal, validation, knowledge generation) lacks technical granularity. The system prompts' quality and design are likely crucial, but details in appendices are not sufficient for a complete assessment.

    *   **Generalizability Concerns:** The LLM-PySC2 environment, while complex, is still a simulated game environment. Whether RAL's performance translates to real-world decision-making scenarios (e.g., robotic control, medical diagnosis) is unclear. This raises questions about external validity.

    *   **Reliance on Existing LLMs:** The performance of RAL heavily depends on the base LLMs used (GPT-3.5, GPT-4, DeepSeek). The paper provides some discussion of robustness across models, but the extent to which RAL can compensate for deficiencies in a "weaker" LLM is unknown. In fact, the paper reveals (surprisingly) that reasoning models perform *worse* than foundation models after RAL. This aspect should be investigated more deeply.
    *   **RAG as a Double-Edged Sword**: While RAG helps LLMs access external knowledge, it is also capable of providing inaccurate or biased information. The paper needs to discuss the RAG-related hallucination problems in more detail.
    *   **Claims May Be Overstated:** While the performance in LLM-PySC2 is promising, the magnitude of the improvements reported may be somewhat overstated, particularly considering the specific (and potentially optimized) experimental setup. The "extremely low cost" claim also requires more scrutiny; while train-free, the repeated LLM calls could still incur significant API costs.
    *   **Limited Comparison To RAG Approaches**: The paper compares RAL to reflection methods, but doesn't include a thorough comparison to RAG-based solutions and state-of-the-art decision making systems. This limits the reader from being able to understand the effectiveness compared to other knowledge-augmented solutions.

*   **Potential Influence:** If the implementation details are sufficiently robust and if the generalizability concerns can be addressed, RAL could influence the development of more efficient and accessible LLM decision-making systems. It could pave the way for deploying LLMs in resource-constrained settings. However, further research and validation are crucial.

**Conclusion:**

The paper presents an interesting and potentially valuable approach. However, the lack of technical depth in the algorithm descriptions, reliance on strong base LLMs, questionability of the performance reported in simulated settings, and limited scope of the evaluation dampen the enthusiasm for this paper.
